PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

A CRANK is a person whose enthusiasm we do not share. "ALL the world loves a lover "—and derives con- siderable amusement from him, too.

"DAR am some men," said Uncle Eben, "dat regards eb'ry change in de weddah as a change foh de wus."

IF, as has erroneously been said, we really profited by our errors, every man-jack of us would be declaring dividends daily. SIISPICIOUS.—Jones :"I have decided to join the Church." Brown: "What have you been doing?" _ _ _ _

HiADING HIM OFF.—Jaspar : "Children often say very funny things." Jumpuppe (guardedly) : " Yes ; but never funny enough to be repeated." HUSBAND: "Now, doctor, tell me candidly, -why is my wife never really well ?" Doctor: "Well, candidly, because she's never really ill 1" THE BENEFIT DERIVED.—Jaspar : "Bighead has studied history critically." Jumpuppe ; "What a great many things he must know to be untrue!"

A QUESTION OF ATTIRE.—" Deah me !" said Chappie. "r weahly don't know what to do. The Alichaux Club is to have a run ahfter eight o'clock to-night, and I don't know how the deuce I'll look on a bicycle in a dress suit !" QUITE CORRECT. —Teacher: "Why are the days so short in the winter ? " Dull Boy " Guess it mus' be 'cause the Mghts are so long." SHE: "This is so sudden! You must ask mamma." He: "Oh, that's all right! She has given me several hints already." THE YOUNG IDEA.—Sunday-school Teacher: "Suppose, my boy, that my watch had no works, and I should sell it to a man for five pounds, what would Ibe ? " Sammy: "A smart business man."

WHAT WAS THE MATTER WITH WASHINGTON? —The latest research makes it appear that George Washington's inability to tell a lie was due to a painful malady now described as "paralysis of mendacity." Latterly the disease has been almost entirely stamped out in America. A WEAK BROTllER.—Theosophist : "How did you like Dr. Faraway's address at our last meet- ing?" Neophyte: "Oh, it was a good enough address, but it was wasn't exactly Theosophic. Why, I understood nearly all of it 1" HER VALUE.—" It's dreadful, really, this new- woman craze, so called," said Robert. "Really, I can't see any good in it." "There's lots of good," retorted Richard. "If it wasn't for the new

woman some of us wouldn't know what a fine, beautiful creature the other woman is."

A LIFE-SAVING PURSUIT.—" A great many lives will be saved by the discovery of the North Pole," said Mr. Bloobumper, in reply to a friend who was discoursing on the usefulness of the search. " How do you make that out?" "It won't be necessary for so many explorers to die in the effort to find it."

A SUNDAY SCHOOL VERSE— The straightest man of ancient times Was Joseph, smooth and slim, Because the King of Egypt made A ruler out of him.

WHAT SHE WAS.—llacket (furiously) : "Do you know, I've married a young woman through a matrimonial agency which claimed her to be a countess, whereas I find she is only a cook 1" Sacket (interestedly) : "A cook! You lucky dog !"

OH, NOTIIINK."—Having taken a cab to his residence, which is near an old cemetery, a gentle- man offered the man eighteenpence, which was just his legal fare, and no more. Cabby looked at the coins, slowly deposited them in his pocket and said, pointing to his horse, " D'ye see that little white 'oss with the short tail ? " " Yes " was the rather puzzled reply. " What about him?" "Oh, nothink ; only I hope the next time you're brought here it'll be a black 'oss with a long tail." HE leaned soulfully nearer. "And could you," he whispered, "think of another?" She cast down her eyes in sweet confusion. "Really," she faltered; "two ices already—er—if you insist— thank you. Yes; strawberry flavour, if you please." THE CORN-FED PHILOSOPHER. "The great trouble with young men who want to see life," remarked the corn-fed philosopher, "is that they imagine that there is none of it worth seeing by daylight.' "TICKETS, PLEASE ! "—A friend of the writer recently engaged a servant who had never been " out " before. A day or two after it happened to be her "At Home" day, but through indisposition she was unable to see her friends, who accordingly left their cards. This proceeding greatly astonished the servant-girl, and determined to get an explana- tion thereof she electrified her mistress by asking— " Must all the ladies show their tickets, ma'am, before I lets 'em in?"

--- --- AT LAST.—The stout one with the eye-glasses gazed with rapture at tile rolling, dark-blue waves. "At last," he said, "the dream of my life is realised ! I have the glories of the mighty deep before my eyes, the moisture of the salt breeze for the first time on my cheek !" Here he wept. The careless loungers of the sandy shore, recognising the celebrated writer of sea stories, respected his emotion, and stood silent, nor jarred upon his joy with their idle babble. DIE TRUSTFULNESS OF LOVE.—" YOU know, dear," said Miss Dalyers, frankly, to her accepted suitor—" you know we get none of papa's money while he lives." "I quite understand that, my precious pet," replied the young man, with the light of love in his eyes. "We will invite him to live with us, put a folding-bed in his room, and hope for the best."

TEACHER: "Do any of you know what is meant by imagination ?" Little Girl: "I do." "Very well. You may define it." "It's w'at boys uses w'en they is asked why they is late to school." No FAR WRONG.—Tommy : "Paw, what is an egotist?" Mr. Figg : "He is a man who thinks he is smarter than any one else." Mrs. Figg : "My dear, you have that wrong. The egotist is the man who says he is smarter than any one else. All men think that way." SPEAKING UP FOR Him.—Briggs "Miss Penn- stock was talking about you last night, and she said she never saw a fellow so calm and immovable as you. She wanted to know if you were ever aroused to sudden action." Griggs: "You stood by me, didn't you ? " Briggs: "Yes. I told her she ought to ask you up to dinner and see." LOVE-TOENS.—Friend "How is your suit with that girl coming on?" Sharpwit : "I haven't had the courage to propose yet; but I know she loves me." " Eli ? How do you know?" "Her father always glares at me when we meet." A LETTER TO HIS MoTHER.-L-A gentleman in Brooklyn was cleaning out his desk the other day and tearing up his old letters, when the packman, a son of Erin, who was in office, spoke up: "Sure, sir, an' ye might gimme one,,of them letters." "What do you want it for? " I promised to i write a letter to my old mithir n Cork, but as I wasn't lamed to write, I can send her one of them letters you hain't got no use for. It'll just make her proud." The gentleman gave the affectionate son a patent medicine anti-fat circular, which was Alulyi_n_Qsted. . _ •

"CAPITAL" CHAT.

The following interesting extract is from a pri- vate letter just received from a well-in- formed Engliah resident in Japan :—" Vur coach- man has been called up to Tokyo for a fortnight's service, without previous not ice,although be served through the war. The army is being supplied with Murata rifles, and the reserves are being called out for practice. The fact is that no only are the peo- ple here very angry with Russia, but they are a little afraid of her as well, and they seem to be pre- paring for possible emergencies.

I have had an opportunity of seeing some of the replies from colonial governors to Mr. Chamberlain's despatch of 28th November last respecting the dis- placement of British by foreign goods in the colonial markets. I noticed in more than one in- stance attention is called to bad packing, and the many breakages and damage resulting therefrom; this it seems is a common complaint with private customers, on whose shout .era the loss falls, and who, consequently, fall back upon local tradesmen suffering from the same complaint. Foreign firms, especially Germans as a rule, pay more attention to the selection and forwarding of goods than do our exporters. One governor offers a very plausible reason for this; he says British shortcomings in that respect are almost entirely due to the substi- tution of the company for the private firm. Servants have not the same interest in the business as em- ployers; boards of directors are too lordly to attend to such a small matter as the packing of goods; in abort, the master's eye is absent.

So tar as I can learn, Mr. George Wyndham's intention to raise the Transvaal question after Easter does not meet with much approval on his own side of the House. It is quite possible that after Easter Mr. Chamberlain may be in a, position to make an important statement in re- gard to Transvaal affairs, but it is known that he regards the situation as still delicate, and as likely to be so for some time, and that ha has no desire to have his hand forced in the matter. A leading Unionist member who has special opportunities for studying the actual situation in South Africa de- clares that things Were progressing quite satis- factorily "if only the busybodies will but refrain from interfering."

The memorial to the Prime Minister and the Chancellor of the Exchequer on the subject of the death duties has been sent in—somewhat prema- turely, it is said. About 203 members of the House of Commons have agreed to it—a much larger number of signatures than the promoters appeared at one time likely to get,—but I believe that some of the members who have signed have made it clear that they do not necessarily commit themselves to a campaign against the duties, al- though they are willing that the promoters should have an opportunity of stating their case.

There has been some discussion recently in Socialist circles as to the •advisability and possi- bility of starting a daily paper to "properly repre- sent the opinions of Socialists "—a somewhat diffi- cult matter—considering how seldom Socialists share tbe same opinions. It is complained that the daily papers sedulously " boycott "Socialist meet- ings by refusing to give them publicity, and this can only be remedied,it is said,by tho establishment of a journal devoted to the chronicling of the do- ings of the party. If we may judge from the re- ports of iany Socialist meetings that reach us, the " party organ" will want a large reserve fund to meet the expense •of defending libel actions. Mr. Tom Mann is of opinion that £15,000 would be suffi- cient to start the paper, a decidedly sanguine eqi- mate if the paper is to be run at all on the same lines as the present dailies, which seems to be the intention.

I hear that some at least of the South American States are by no means enthusiastic over that de- velopment of the Monroe doctrine which found ex- pression in Mr. Olnev's famous despatch and in President Cleveland's still more famous Message. In Chili especially there is a disposition to repudi- ate the virtual claim 04 f the United States to control the foreign relations of that Republic. The appli- cation of the Monroe doctrine to South America is regarded with disfavourais it is held that it would be but" the thin end of the wedge," giving the statesmen at Washington a claim to still further in- terference should they ever deem it desirable. In a. word, the States of South America look with jeal- ousy on a policy which might tamper with their absolute independence. They do not want to en- courage or even to tolerate the idea that there is any sort of protectorate of the southern continent vested in the United States.

It is said that. great efforts will be made to make the dinner that is to !le given by the National Liberal Clubin honour of Sir William Harcourt, a big succeßs. From what I hear, the list of applica- tions for tickets is likely to be exceedingly large.

The rising in Matabeleland is in all political'and financial cparters regarded as a very serious event, even though some progress has been made in put- ting it down.• One of its worst features is that it is not the result of a sudden impulse either ef fanatic- ism or T a'riotism, for there are symptoms enough to prose it to be the result of a conspiracy which has been concocted in secrecy, and was utterly un- suspected by the English authorities. It is easy to trace it to the effect produced upon the mind of the natives by the defeat of Jameson. and to wild ru• mours as to the relations of the English and Dutch in the Rand. Whether it is the work of the native police or not, one is tempted to ask how it could be possible that Englishmen, with their vast experi- ence of the methods of native races, had no secret machinery in hand for keeping themselves informed as to the state of native feeling—an intelligence de- partment, or, in less eunhemistic phraseology, an organised spy system. There appears also to have been remissness'in organising a proper force to take the place of Jameson's captured troopers, and yet that should have been the first duty of the Im- perial Government after it assumed ,the responsi- bility for the peace and good conduct of the new

• territory. City men here would have been prepared to face a renewal of the misunderstanding with Germany if Mr. Chamberlain could only have been able to announce at the end of last. week that the purchase of the Delagoa Bay from Portugal was an ACCOMD- lished fact. The rumour was credited in political and colonial circles, by financiers, and in military club., and the idea is by no means departed from that an effort has been made by this country to in- duce Pprtugal to sell. And the•conviction is equally strong that some day, sooner or later, Portugal will be tempted to hart with her rights over this coveted harbour. •

The Church Association has determined to ask Parliament for a commission of inquiry into the Church appointments of the Prime Minister and the bishops. A petition is now in course of signature calling the attention of the House of Commons to the spread of Ritualism in the Church. It appears from this document that of the thirty-four bishops no fewer than twenty belong to the High Church or "Ritualistic" school,while only five are Evangeli- cals. It is alleged that Lord Salisbury is largely responsible for this; at any rate, he has made thir- teen High Church and only three Evangelical prelates.

I learn from private sources in Cairo that an im- pression widely prevalent in the European colonies in Egypt disconnects England altogether from the initiative of the Dongola expedition. If the view ! largely held in those quarters is correct, a number 1 of questions not precisely reassuring for this coun- try at once suggest themselves. The initiative is believed to have been taken by Germany and .11s- ' tria. It has been "at the instance" of these two countries, according to the local opinion I am quot- ing. that the British Government resolved originally to "operate a diversion first at Wady Haifa and afterwards at Suakim to disengage the Italian gar- rison at Kassala." I hear at the same time—and my Cairo informant is not a partisan of evacuation —that "if we may judge from appearances the Agfince Britannique here was not consulted as to the forward movement in principle." Slatin Pasha, by the way, has just received from the Emperor of Austria a special medal, bestowed upon him by His ' Majesty in acknowledgment of a copy of " Fire and Sword in the Soudan."

All rights reserved. THIN GS A WOMAN WANTS TO KNOW.

AT the present time all the boys and girls are at home for the holidays, and this means a busy time for those who have to provide for their wardrobes. The fashion of coats and skirts is an exceedingly convenient one for girls.• A suit of this kind made of a good blue serge will stand any amount of hard wear, and will look well to the last. With regard to the style, I think there is nothing better than a reefer coat, with a skirt of medium width. 6 In my first sketch I am showing you a charming design for one of these useful little garments suitable for a child of from ten to twelve. The original was fashioned of a very dark navy blue serge of medium thickness,

_ To am had from PATTERN DEPART/4E17;18, HENRIETTA. Smarr, LONDON, W.C.

and was lined with scarlet Italian cloth. This, with a variety of shirts, would be all that a girl could require for daily use from now to the end of the summer.

I think girls' dress was never more sensible than it is at the present time, nor eould it be more economical. Any lady who is fond of needlework, and who has time to bestow on it, can make all her daughters' clothes with very little trouble, provided, of course, that she secures good Ratterns to start with. I think it is an excellent plan for girls to include in their wardrobe one or two shirts of very thin flannel. A tennis flannel of either pale blue or pink is charming for the purpose, and, provided the sleeves are of moderate dimensions only, two and a quarter or two and a half yards will be ample for the purpose. Shirts of this kind are splendid for boating or tennis; in fact for all kinds of outdoor games. They can be worn with a linen collar, which perhaps looks better than one of ordinary flannel. I have recently been assisting in the manu- facture of the trousseau of a friend of mine. She is not blessed with a superabundance of this world's goods, but is determined, in spite of that, to have a nice and useful trousseau. She is, consequently, doing a great deal of the work herself, and is spending all the money she can on good materials. When I was with her the other day she was making some night- dresses, and I was charmed to see how pretty and how simple they were. Even though I make my own underclothes, I have always looked upon a nightdress as such a very big undertaking, with all its tucks, insertion, .and so on, that I have generally pre- ferred to buy these garments ready-made.

A Pretty Nightdress. No. 1993. Paper Pattern, 81d. To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT, 18, HENRIETTA STREET, LONDON, W.C. - -

However, I shall certainly not do so again, but shall make them for myself, simply and prettily as my friend did hers. I sketched one for you, so that you may copy it if you like. ' The material was a fine longcloth. In design it had a small saddle back to strengthen it, which was concealed by the deep collar. The front of the nightdress was full, being just tucked a little on either side of the waist. The front opening had a band of the longcloth, feather-stitched on either side with coarse crotchet cotton. This and the deep sailor collar were edged with a pretty- embroidered frill simply "whipped" at the edge and sewn on. You will notice that the front frill is gradu- ated, being much wider at the top than at the bottom. A band of insertion to match was laid on round the collar. The sleeves were large and full, and of the bishop shape, but gathered into a band of insertion at the wrist with a frill of embroidery below. Some of my friend's nightdresses were made in this way, and others were made from the same pattern, but instead of having a sailor collar, had a turn-down Toby frill of the em- broidery. A variation of the embroidered frill might be made by choosing a woven frilling (which can be purchased with a thread in it all ready to draw up) edged with a narrow torchon lace. This is decidedly pretty, and is almost indestructible, even in the hands of the up-to-date laundress. Of course, if you are used to sleeping in flannel, my pattern would still answer exceed- ingly well, for frills of embroidered flannel could take the place of those of a lighter make.

I have just found such a delightfully simple remedy for a burn that it is worth your while making a note of it. Cover the affected part with some scraped raw potato, lay a piece of rag over it, and then bind with a bandage. The pain will be almost immediately relieved. Another good remedy is to apply a lotion of equal parts of lime-water and linseed oil. Now that the sun is gaining strength daily, you may be glad to know a good recipe for the removal of sunburn. Put a handful of parsley in a teapot and pour over it a pint of boiling water. Let it infuse, and when cool bathe the face with the tea, when the disfiguring marks of tan will disappear. The warm days have made me turn out the wicker garden chairs from the recesses of the loft, and I find that spring sunshine shows up most ruthlessly all their imperfections. g I am, therefore,4Vtaining them with a mixture of permanganate of potash (half an ounce in a quart of boiling water) which makes them a rich brown colour. The next thing to he done is to varnish them, when I anticipate their looking as good as new again.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY APRIL 4. 1896 THE HIDDEN HAND;

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Queen has abandoned her intention of going to Coburg to attend the wedding of Princess Alexandra and the Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe-Laugenburg, and according to present arrangements her Majesty will return to Windsor Castle about the end of the month and reside there until Whitsuntide. Princess Henry of Battenberg and her children will return to Windsor Castle with the Queen. Prince Alexander, Princess Beatrice's eldest son, is shortly to join his cousin the Duke of Albany at a school in the New Forest. The Queen and the Princess will doubtless both feel parting with the little Prince, as he has never been away from either one or the other. The young Duke of Albany who by the way is a merry, sweet-dispositioned little fellow, will be entered as a student at Eton next year, and in due time go to the University. The three eldest sons of the German Emperor are to go almost immediately to the cadet-school at Ploen. where they are to continue their education. Six other cadets will join the three Princes in their military studies, three of them are the sons of noblemen, and three are commoners. Thus each Prince has for his companions two cadets of different social standing. The three Imperial beys are looking forward with great expectation to their life at Ploen and to the companionship of other boys. The special honour always paid to Prince and Princess Christian by the German Emperor and Empress,surprises many observers, until they look back upon events which shew them that this appreciation and regard for the Royal occupiers of Cumberland Lodge is not only natural, but the outcome of personal gratitude. Long before the German Empress had any idea of attaining her present exalted position, she, when Princess Augusta of Schleswig-Holstein, poor and somewhat neglect,d by , her family, received the greatest kindnesses from her English-living relatives,and this fact has never been for- gotten by the Emperor and Empress:

For Miss Marie Corelli is claimed the distinction of having produced the most successful book of the season. Mudie's took 2,000 "Sorrows of Stan" to start with. Miss Corelli, however, is not altogether in favour in Middlesbrough. The Free Library Committee there has just decided to exclude "The Sorrows of Satan " from their shelves. Some of the members confessed that they had not read the work, others had read it,and corisid,..red it too "outspoken," while one of the championo of the book " believed it would be read when half of the ep- hemeral literature of the day was forgotten." This latter opinion however, failed to find universal acceptance, and the book has been denied admittance.

At the forthcoming exhibition at the Royal Academy only cne picture by the late president will, in all pro- bability, be shown, for although Lord Leighton, as was his custom,was engaged upon several works concurently there was only one finished at the time of his death. Tte several unfinished pictures, together with his studies and pictures, will be put up to auction at Tokenhouse- yard in June, when the late President's house in Flo!. land Park-road, and all his effects, are to be disposed of. Several additions to the National Gallery have been made of late. Among them is an equestrian portrait of William 111. and a Crucifixion of Spinello Aretino,which has been hung in the vestibule among the early Italians. A still life of Willem H. Hedu will shortly be hung in room 10., and a small battle scene of the rather rare master. Jacob Weier, of the school of Wouverman, will soon fled a place among the Dutch pictures. A supple- mentary catalogue of painting acquired since the public- ation of the last edition of the Foreign and British cata- logue up to the end of 1895, will be ready shortly. It seems probable that,in spite of recent sad events,we are to have a full and brilliant season. Houses are being quickly snapped up in the West End and the best dress- makers are as busy as bees,another excellent sign. After Easter, gaieties and festivities will commence, and con- tinue with only a short break for Whitsuntide, right up to the end of July, which summer month will be ur.- usually brilliant owing totbe marriage of Princess Maud of Wales with Prince Charles of Denmark. For this cere- mony there will be many Royal visitors to London and previous to it doubtless a command night at the opera. The marriage of Miss Maple to Baron von Eckardstein is to take place on April 15th at St. Albans Abbey, and promises to be a very fashionable affair. A special train for the wedding guests,numhering several hundreds.will start from St. Pancras about noon and carriages will be provided from them at St. Albans. After the ceremony and luncheon at Sir Blundell Mapel's country seat,Child- wickburv, the guests will all be conveyed back to town by another special train.

It appears that wedding rings for men are slowly coming into fashion here as they are in several other countries, and frequently they are engraved with a motto inside. This latter idea is a revival of a pretty oid custom during the sixteenth and seventeenth cen- tury, even as late as last century, of having a motto or verse engraved inside a wedding or betfothal ring, the motto being called the" posy" or "chanson." Some of the posies were quaint, others tenth r,some of a hum. orous nature. The then Bishop of Linzoln in 1753, on marrying his fourth wife, had the following motto en- graved in his wedding ring, "If I survive I'll make them five," which was hardly complimentary to the lady. In the ring which Willis mof Orange gave to Princess Mary is engraved the Royal motto and the line, "I'll win and wear you if I can," and Louis IV. of Franze had his ring engraved with fleurs des us in com- pliment to his wife Marguerite. Loose Saque coats are greatly in evidence. The round shouldered however, would do well to avoid them, as upon these particular individuals a hump-backed appear- ance is generally the result. 'I he yoke into which the full folds are set is often very elaborately trimmed with embroidery and braiding. The central pleat is usually much wider than those on either side of it, and it is sometimes also much more prominent. Many of the new sacque coats have but one pleat in the back and two in front. Sometimes the latter are set with a yoke, but more often they fall sheer from the neck. The coats are quite short, not measuring more than three quarters of a yard in length. The liaing is usually a good brocade but very often a surati or striped silk is substituted. It is a mistake however, to choose one with a poor lining, It wears out almost immediately. For early race meetings some very lovely dust cloaks in grenadine, chin ior chameleon silks, shot in three colors, are being prepared, with a cape, like sleeves, be- ruched, and be-frilled, with either silk-edged chiffon or fine black lace, some having huge upstanding collars lined with soft laces, others are ruffled at the neck with velvet bows and chiffon.

Although plain, skirts need careful adjustment just now to give them the right hang, and above all things they should clear the ground all round for walking, and not dip or droop anywhere. 'I he most usual shape now is a narrow front, two side gores, and one or two back widths, according to whether the skirt is wanted very wide or no. A small amount of horsehair interlining is still used to set out the base usually about ten inches in front and fourteen in the back. In addition a strip of whalebone is often inserted quite at the edge of the front width to set it out well from the feet. Having joined up the skirt it should be set into the band, aud then put on to a stand, so that the hem may be regulated, otherwise it is impossible to insure that it will hang straight. Elastic bands inside are used to keep the godet folds in place. For some ten or twelve years now high corsets have been worn, pushing up the bust as high as in the days of the First Empire. Now it seems our feminine love of change after a decade of constancy, begins to grow weary. Low stays are the only correct wear at the nsoment,with low bust.the corsets catching in the figure at the waist, and expanding again on the hips. If my reader; would appear very up-to-date, they must wear one gigantic blossom well planted on the corsage. It will cover the frout of their gown almost but they must not mind. A poppy should be chosen, or a wild rose, a violet or a single dahlia. It should be worn at receptions, either day or evening. EiEDGAREE " is a capital way whereby to USA up scraps ot cold fish for breakfast. First hard boil an egg and place it in cold water till ready to use it. Next melt half-an-ounce of butter, and add to it a quarter of a pound of boiled rice and the same quantity of any cold fish. Cut up the white of the hard boiled egg into small pieces and add it also, then season well with pepper and salt, and make it quite hot. Have ready a hot dish and pile the fish, etc., up roughly on it. Garnish with chopped parsley and the yoke of an egg passed through a sieve or chopped very fine. . _

A the Liverpool Assizes on Monday a Roumanian named Marcus Weintach,who had previously been found guilty of an attempt to procure two young Austrian girls for immoral purposes, was brought up for sentence. The prisoner, under the device of finding them situations as domestic servants in South America, had brought the girls from Austria, and it was proved to the satisfcc- tion of the jury that his real object was to send them to Buenos Ayres for a different purpose. Mr. Justice Ken- nedy sentenced the prisoter to twelve months' hard labour.

CHAPTER XXX. THE ORPHAN. Let me die, father ! I fear, I fear To fall in earth's terrible strife !" Not so, my child, for the crown must be won In the battle-field of life." _

—LIFE AND DEATH. "He has gone to sleep again," said Clara, with a sigh of relief. "lie has gone to Heaven, my child," said Marah Roche, softly. The orphan started, gazed wildly on the face of the dead, turned ghastly pale, and with a low moan and suffocating sob, fell fainting into the motherly arms of Mrs. Rocke. Marah beckoned Traverse, who lifted the in- sensible girl tenderly in his arms, and preceded by his mother, bore her to her chamber and laid her upon the bed. Then Marah dismissed Traverse to attend to the duties owed to the remains of the beloved departed, while she herself staid with Clara, using every means for her restoration.

Clara opened her eyes at length, but in reviving to life also returned to grief. Dreadful to witness was the sorrow of the orphan girl. She had con- trolled her grief in the presence of her father, and while he lingered in life, only to give way now to its overwheiming force. Marah remained with her, holding her in her arms, weeping with her, praying for her, doing all that the most tender mother could do to soothe, console and strengthen the bleeding yowl.; heart. The funel al of Dr. Day took place the third day from his decease, and was attended by all the gentry of the neighbouring town and the county, in their own carriages, and by crowds who came on foot to pay the last tribute of respect to their be- loved friend.

He was interred in the family burial ground, situated on a wooded hill up behind the homestead, and at the head of his last restiug-place was after- wards erected a plitin aelisk of white marble, with his name and the date of his birth and death, and the following inscription : "115 Is NOT HERE, BUT IS RISEN."

" When dear Clara comes to weep at her father's grave, these words will send her away comforted, and with her faith renewed," had been Traverse Rocke's secret thouelit, when giving directions for the inscription of this inspiring text. On the morning of the day succeeding the funeral, while Clara, exhausted by the violence of her grief, lay prostrate upon her chamber cough, Mrs. Rocke and Traverse sat conversing in that once pleasant, now desolate, morning reading-room. " You know, dear mother, that by the doctor's desire, which should be considered sacred, Clara is still to live here, and you are to remain to take care of her. I shall defer my journey West, until everything is sett:ed to Clara's catisfaction, and she has in some degree recovered her equanimity. I must also have an interview and a good under- standing with her guardian, for whom I ha 'e a message." "Who is this guardian of whom I have heard you speak more than once, Traverse ? " asked Marah.

"Dear mother, will you believe me that I have forgotten the man's name; it was an uncommon name that I never heard before in my life, and, in the pressure of grief upon my mind, its exact identity escaped my memory ; but that does not signify much, as he is expected hourly; and when he announces himself, either by card or word of mouth, I shall know, for I shall recognise the name the moment I see it written or hear it spoken. Let me see—it was something like Des Moines, De Vaugn, De Saule—or something of that sort. At all events, I'm sure I shall know it again the instant I see or hear it. And now, dear mother, I must ride up to Staunton to see some of the doctor's poor sick, that he left in my charge for as long as I stay here. I shall be back by three o'clock. I need not ask you to take great care of that dear suffering girl up-stairs," said Traverse, taking his hat and gloves for a ride. "I shall go and stay with her as soon as she wakes," answered Mrs. Rocke. And Traverse, satisfied, went his way. He had been gone perhaps an hour, when the sound of a carriage was beard below in the front of the house, followed soon by a loud rapping at the hall door.

"It is dear Clara's guardian," said Marah Rocke, rising and listening. Soon a servant entered and placed a card in her hand, saying: "The gentleman is waiting in the hall below, and asked to see the :person that was in charge here. ma'am. So I fotch the card to you." "You did right, John. Show the gentleman up here," said Mara,h ; and as soon as the servant had gone she looked at the card, but failed to make it out. The name was engraved in Old English text„ and in such a complete labyrinth, thicket and net- work of ornate flourishes, that no one who was not familiar at once with the name and the style could possibly have distinguished it. "I do not think my boy would know this name at sight !" was Marah's thought, as she twirled the card in her hand, and stood waiting the entrance of the visitor, whose step was now heard coming up the stairs. Soon the door was thrown open, and the stranger entered. Marali, habitually shy in the presence of strangers, dropped her eyes before she had fairly taken in the figure of a tall, handsome, dark complexioned, dis- tinguished looking man somewhat past middle age, and arrayed in a rich military cloak, and carrying in his hand a military cap. The servant who admitted him had scarcely re- tired, when Marah looked up, and her eyes and those of the stranger met—and—

" MARAII ROCK! ! ! "COLONEL LE Nom ! !! "

Burst simultaneously from the lips of each.

Le Noir first recovered himself, Ilnd holding out bottt hands, advanced towards her with a smile as if to greet an old friend. But Marah, shrinking from him in, horror, turhed and tottered to the farthest window, where leaning her head against the sash, she moaned: "Oh, my heat t I my heart I is this the wolf to whom my lamb must be committed?" As she moaned these words, she was aware of a soft step at her side and a low voice murmuring: " Marah Rocke, yes! the same beatriful Marah that as a girl of fifteen, twenty years ago, turned my head, led me by her fatal charms into the very jaws of death ! the same lovely Marali with her beauty only ripened by time and exalted by sor- row."

With one surprised, indignant look, but without a word of reply, Mrs. Rocke turned and walked composedly towards the door with the intention of quitting the room. fleolonel Le Noir saw and forestalled her purpose by springing forward, turning the key, and stand- ing before the door. "Forgive me, Marah, but I must have a word with you before we part," he said, in those soft, sweet, persuasive tones he knew so well how to assume.

Marah remembered that she was an honourable matron and an honoured mother, that as such, fears and tremors and self-distrust in the presence of a villain, would not well become her; so calling up all the gentle dignity latent in her nature, she resumed her seat, and signing to the visitor to follow her example, she said, composedly: "Speak on, Colonel Le Noir,—remembering, if you please, to whom you speak." "I do remember, Marah remember but too well !"

"They call me Mrs. Rocke who converse with me, sir."

" Marah, why this resentment ? Is it possible that you can sja be angry? Have I remained true to my attachment all these years, and sought you thro4ghout hs wurld to find this reeepLion last ?"

"Colonel Le Noir, if this is all you had to say it was scarcely worth while to have detained me," said Mrs. Rocke, calmly. " But it is not all, my Marah. Yes, I eel you nthie by virtue of the strongest attaclnnent man ever felt for woman. March Rocke, yoc were the only woman who ever inspired me wita a feeling worthy to be-called a passion—" "Colonel to Noir, how dare you blaspheme this house of mourning by such sinful word:, ! You forget where you stand and to whom you speak." "I forget nothi-og, Marah Rocke, nor do I violate his sanctuary of sorrow,"—here he sunk his voice below his usual low tones when. I i.peak of the passion that maddened my youth and 'withered my manhoo I—a passion whose intensity was its excuse for all extravagances and whose enduring constancy is its final, full justification." Before he had finished this sentence, Marah Rocke had c.tholy riscn and pulled the bell, rope. " What mean yea by that, March 2" he in- quired. Before she replied, a servant, in answer to the 1.2e11, came_ to the door .and tried the latch; and,

finding it locked, rapped. With a blush that mounted to his forehead, and with a half-suppressed imprecation, Colonel Le Noir went and unlocked the door, and admitted the man. "John," said Mrs. Rocke, quietly, "show Col- onel Le Noir to the apartment prepared for him, and wait his orders." And, with a slight nod to the guest, she went calmly from the room. Colonel Le Noir, unmindful of the presence of the servant, stood gazing in angry mortification after her. The flush on his brow had given way to the fearful pallor of rage or hate, as he muttered inaudibly: "Insolent beggar! contradiction always con- firms my half-formed resolutions; years ago I swore to possess that woman, and I will do it, if it be only to keep my oath and humble her inso- lence. She is very handsome still; she shall be my slave." Then, perceiving the presence of John, he said : "Lead the way to my room, sirrah, and then go and order my fellow to bring up my portmanteau." John devoutely pulled his forelock as he bowed ! low, and then went out, followed by Colonel Le Noir. Marah Roche meanwhile had gained the privacy of her own chamber, where all her firmness deserted her. Throwing herself into a chair, she ellsped her hands and sat with blanched face and staring eyes, like a marble statue of despair. "Oh, what shall I do ? while this miscreant remains here? this villain whose very presence desecrates the roof and dishonours me? I would instantly leave the house but that I must not abandon poor Clara. "I csinist claim the protection of Traverse, for I would net provoke him to wrath or run him into danger; nor indeed would I even permit my son to dream such a thing possible as that his mother could receive insult. "Nor can I warn Clara of the unprincipled char- acter of her guardian, for if she knew him as he is, she would surely treat him in such a way as to get his enmity—nis dangerous, fatal enmity—doubly fatal since her person and property are legally at his disposal. Oh, my dove ! my dove ! that you should be in the power of this vulture. Whatshall I do, oh, Heaven!" Marah dropped on her knees and finished her soliloquy with prayer. Then, feeling composed and strengthened, she went to Clara's room. She found the poor girl lying awake and quietly weeping. "Your guardian has arrived, love," she said, sitting down beside the bed and taking Clara's hand. "Oh, must I get up and dress to see a stranger?" sighed Clara, wearily. "No, love, you need not stir until it is time to dress for dinner; it will answer quie well if you meet your guardian at table," said Marah, who had particular reasons for wishing that Clara should first see Colonel Le Noir with other company to have an opportunity of observing him well and possibly forming an estimate of his character (as a young girl of her fine instincts might well do) before she should be exposed in a tete-a-tete to tho,se deceptive blandishments he knew so well how to bring into play. "That is a respite I Oh, dear Mrs. Rocke, you don't know how I dread to see any one !" "My dear Clara, you must combat grief by prayer, which is the only thing that can overcome it," said Marah. Mrs. Rocke remained with her young charge as long as she possibly could, and then she went down stairs to oversee the preparation of the dinner. And it was at the dinner table that Marah, with the quiet and gentle dignity for which she was distinguished, introduced the younger members of the family to the guest, in these words: "Your ward, Miss Day, Colonel Le Noir." The Colonel bowed deeply, and raised the hand of Clara to his lips, murmuring some sweet, soft, silvery and deferentially inaudible words of con- dolence, sympathy, and melancholy pleasure, from which Clara, with a gentle bend of her head, with- drew to take her seat. "Colonel Le Noir, my son, Dr. Rocke," said Marah, presenting Traverse. The colonel stared superciliously, bowed with ironical depth, said he was "much honoured," and turning his back on the young man, placed himself at the table. During the dinner he exerted himself to be agreeable to Miss Day and Mrs. Roche, but Tra- verse he affected to treat with supercilious neglect, or ironical deference. Our young physician had too much self-respect to permit himself to be in any degree affected by this rudeness. And Marah, on her part, was glad, so that it did not trouble Traverse, that Le Noir should behave in this manner, so that Clara should be enabled to form some correct idea of his dis- position. When dinner was over, Clara excused herself and retired to her room, whither she was soon followed by Mrs. Roche. "Well, my dear, bow do you like your guardian?" asked Marah, in a tone as indifferent as she could make it. "I do not like him at all," exclaimed Clara, her gentle blue eyes flashing with indignation through their tears ; " I do not like him at all, the scornful, arrogant, supercilious— —Oh! I do not wish to use such strong langus.ge, or to grow angry when I am in such deep grief; but my dear father could not have known this man, or he never would have chosen him for my guardian 1 do you think he would, Mrs. Roche?" "My dear, your excellent father must have thought well of him, or he would never have in- trusted him with so precious a charge. Whether your father's confidence in this man will be justified as far as you are concerned, time will show. Meanwhile, my love, as the guar- dian appointed by your father, you should treat him with respect ; but so far as re- posing any trust in him goes, consult your own instinct s "I shall! and I thank heaven that I have not got to go and live with Colonel Le Noir !" said Clara, fervently. Mrs. Rocke sighed. She remembered that the arrangement that permitted Clara to live at her own home with her chosen friends was but a verbal one, not binding upon the guardian and executor, unless he chose to consider it so. Their conversation was interrupted by the en- trance of a servant with a message from Colonel Le Noir, expressing a hope that Miss Day felt better for her afternoon's repose, and desiring the favour of her company in the library. Clara returned an answer pleading indisposition, and begging upon that account to be excused. At tea, however, the whole family met again. As before, Colonel Le Noir exerted himself to please the ladies, and treated the young man with marked neglect. This conduct offended Miss Day to such a decree that she, being a girl of truth in thought, word' and deed, could only exhibit towards the guest the most freezing politeness that was con- sistent with her position as hostess, and she longed for the time to come that should deliver their peaceful home and loving little circle from the un- welcome presence of this arrogant intruder. "How can he imagine that I can be pleased with his deference and courtesy and elaborate compli- ments, wl.en he permitshimself to be so rude to Traverse? I hope Traverse will tell him of our engagement, which will., perhaps, suggest to him the propriety of reforming his manners, while he remains under a roof of which Traverse is the des- tined master I" said Clara to herself, as she arose from the table, and with a cold bow, turned to retire from the room. "Anti will not my fair ward give me a few hours of her company this evening ?" inquired Colonel Le Noir, in an insinuating voice, as he took and pre,sed the hand of the doctor's orphan daughter. "Excuse me, sir; but except at meal times, I have not left my room sin e "—here her voice broke down—she could not speak to him of her bereave- ment, or give way in his presence, to her holy sorrow. "Besides, sir," she added, "Doctor Roche, I know, has expressed to you his desire for an early interview." "My fair young friend, Doctor Rocke, as you style the young man, will please be so condescend- ing as to tarry the leisure of his most humble servant," replied the colonel, with an ironical bow in the direction of Traverse. "Perhaps, sir, when you know that Doctor Roche is charged with the last uttered will of my dear father, and that it is of more importance than you are prepared to anticipate, you may be willing to favour Us all by granting this young man' an early audience," said Clara. "The last uttered will! I had supposed that the will of my late brother in-law was regularly drawn up and executed and in the hands of his confidential attorney at Staunton." "Yes, sir, so it is; but I refer to my father's last dying wishes, his verbal directions entrusted to his confidential friend, Doctor Roche," said Clara. "Last verbal directions entrusted to Doctor .Rocke. Humph humph ! this would require cor- roborative evidence," said the colonel. 'Such corroborative evidence can be had, sir."

said Clara, coldly "and as I know that Doctiig Rocke has already requested an interview for the sake of an explanation of these subjects, I nitv4 also join my own request to his, and assure yeti that by giving him an early opportunity of coming to an understanding with you, you will greatli oblige me." "Then, undoubtedly, my sweet young friend, your wishes shall be commands—Eh! you—sir Doctor—What's-your name! meet me in the librarY at ten o'clock to-morrow morning," said Le Noir, insolently. "I have engagements, sir, that will occupy lee between the hours of ten and three—before or after that period I am at your disposal," said Traverse, coldly. " Pardieu ! It seems to me that /am placed at yours !" replied the colonel, lifting his eyebrows ; "but as I am so placed by the orders of my fair. little tyrant here, so be it !—at nine to-morrow am your most obedient servant !" "At nine then, sir, I shall attend yon," Baia Traverse with a cold bow. Clara slightly courtesied and withdrew from the room, attended by Mrs. Rocke. Traverse, as the only representative of host, re- mained for a short time with his uncourteous guest, who, totally regardless of his presence, threw hire' self into an arm chair, lighted a cigar, took up a book, and smoked and read.

Whereupon Traverse, seing this, withdrew to the library to employ '' .self with finishing the arranging and tying n (13)-f certain papers, left to his charge by Doctorlja ,y. (To oe continued.)

GREAT LOSS OF LIFE.

Hardly a day goes by without one seeing in broad letters, on the bulletins, " Great loss of life," " Appal? ing disaster." What with fire-traps in London ; reckless' ness or carelessness at sea, cyclones in America, land' slides in Japan, and volcanoes in Italy,no city, no courr try, no continent seems exempt. There is, however, 10 most instances, a summary judical inquiry, and ever/ effort is made to minimise the cnances for a repitititiod as well as alleviate the suffering victims. There Is one source of danger which claims hundredo of victims in all lands, and which, if taken by statistical' causes more deaths than any one of the above disastera, and with regard to which, strange to say, there la general apathy. That is, the danger resulting frord ignoring the seriousness of a cold settling in the kidneya• An aching back ; fickle appetite ; nervousness, sleep- lessness, langour ; a slight puffiness of the eyelids :a stitch under the shoulder-blades, and occasional brick. dust deposit in the urine are the forerunners of kidnel disease, which, if not arrested, will prove fatal. • Mr. A. Underwood, Wells Street, Camberwell, S.E., says any in January, 1895, I took a slight cold. which, however, seemed confined to the head, not suffi' cient to cause an anxiety on the part of a young man in the prime of life, as I am. Two or three days after wards I noticed a little swelling in one of my feet. Nelt week the other foot commenced to swell. I noticed my' back seemed weak, and I was suffering from a dull, heavy pain in the small of the back; the water wag darker than usually passed. Suddenly my eyelids and face showed signs of swelling, and my throat became swollen. I had no idea what the trouble was, but wal so frightened I determined to get the best advice poa• sible, and immediately went under treatment at one of the leading London hospitals. The doctors prescribed medicines to reduce the dropsical swelling, but failing 10 their effort they resorted to hot vapour baths in addi• tion to the medical treatment. The physicians in charge said I had Bright's Disease of the Kidneys, and after 17: ing in the hospital twenty-one weeks. I was discharged incurable. Fortunately, I heard of, and determined te try, Warner's Safe Cure. I took it faithfully for a month before I noticed any improvement. Then there seemed a lessening of the dropsical swelling; the water passed more freely and naturally; then the dropsical swelling commenced to rapidly decrease, and my con' valescence from this time was uninterrupted. The health which I now enjoy, after such a tedious and seri' ous illness, is quite sufficient to make me very grateful. and for that and no other reason I give this letter pub. licity. If any doubt my wonderful rescue, the facts can be verified by calling or writing,"

ONLY A LITTLE AT A TIME.

There are sound objections to one's knowing too much abcut his own body. I am going to tell you what they are ;.not tc-day, but soon. To make sure of them

you will have to watch these articles sharply in the

newspapers. Yet we should know a little ; and a frac- tion of that little I will serve up n ,w. 'lease favour me with your attention. Right across the middle of the body is a large, thin flat muscle, stretched like a canvass awning—the diaph- ragm. By it you are divided into two large storeys or compartments.

The upper one contains the heart and ungs, the lower one contains (chiefly) the stomach, the intestines, and the liver. The most painful (internal) diseases occur downstairs, the least painful upstairs.

The entire right side of the lower compartment, from the top down to the short ribs, is filled by the liver,

which is suspended to a mere point of the diaphragm and shakes about with every movement you make. Now, from the location of the liver we have a word used for ages to express one of the most unhappy condi- tions a human being can fall into—the word hypochon- dria (often abbreviated " hpo "), the word meaning under the cartilages.

"For seven years," writes a correspondent, "I suffered from complaint of the liver. I was very bili- ous, my skin was sallow and dry, and the whites of my eyes yellow. I had much pain and weight at my right side, and was constantly depressed and melancholy. It seemed to be out of my power to take a hopeful or cheer- ful view of anything. The effect of this complaint on thelmind was one of the aspects of it,the hardest to bear. " I had lost my natural appetite, and ate to support life ; but there was no more any genuine relish for food or drink. The bad taste in my mouth made all that I took taste bad. Sometimes I would be taken sick and throw up all I had eaten ; and after a meal, no matter how slender and simple, I was troubled with fulness and pain at the chest. I used many kinds of medicines and while some of them may have relieved me for the mom- ent,none conferred any lasting benefit,and I was soon as bad as ever.

' In March, 1892, I read in a small book of what Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup had done in eases similar to mine, and was especially interested in the account given in the book of the nature and duties of the liver, and its disorders. I gc.,t a bottle of the Syrup from B, ots' Drug Stores, and after taking it a few days felt quite like a new man. It seemed to correct my stomach and

liver and clear my system of all bile ; and it left me in capital haalth. Since that time I have kept Mother Seigel's Syrup in the house as a family medicine and have commended it to all my friends as the best known cure for ailments like the one from which I suffered SO miserably and so long. You can use this statement as you like. (Signed) John Gent, 59, Coventry-road, Bul- well, Nottingham, March 21st, 1895." "In the spring of 1891," writes another, " I found myself in bad health. I had no appetite, and the little I did eat did me no good. gave me no strength. I had great pain and weight at the chest and right side, and my skin turned sallow and dry. My kidneys also acted badly, and from time to time I had attacks of gravel ; and cold, clammy, weakening sweats broke out all over me. Being only seventeen years old when the trouble began I was greatly alarmed and anxious. No doctor was able to help nre, and I continued thus for over three years. In June, 1894, I began to use Mother Seigel's Syrup and soon felt•better, , lighter, and more cheerful. And by taking it a few weeks longer I recovered my health and strength. Since then, when I have any stomach, liver, or kidney symptoms I resort to Mother Seigel's Syrup and it never fails to set me right. You can publish this letter. (Signed) C. Hanson, 6, New Inn Lane, Gloucester, May 31st, 1895." The stomach, the liver, and the kidneys are all con- nected parts of the food and digestive system. When disordered (usually through torpidity of the stomach) they cripple the body and throw a gloom as of night over the mind. On the earliest signs of anything wrong with them use Mother Seigel's Syrup at once.

WARRINGTON INFIRMARY AND DISPENSARY.

ANNUAL MEETING OF. GOVERNORS.

The annual general meeting of the Governors ot the Warrington Infirmary was held in the Masonic Hall, Bold-st., on Thursday, last week. Mr. J. C. Parr pre- sided. There were also present :—Messrs. John Fair- dough, J. Roberts, J. Taylor, G. Thompso a, Pickmere, V. Davies, J. White, G. Dakin, H. Thornton, and F. Monks: Drs. J. Gornall, Richmond, C. White, Fox, Joseph, T. S. Smith, Langdale ; and the Revs. Canon Willis, H. V. Pigot, W. H. Drummond, J. Davies, and B. Percival; Mr. J. L. Tunstall (secretary), and C. Leigh (assistant secretary). ALTERATION OP RULES. A large number of the rules were amended. On reading rule 92, and the proposed amendment, several gentlemen objected to the amendment. The rule and amendment read as follows. Rule 92 :—" No per- son shall be admitted as an out-patient who is in receipt of parochial relief, or whose weekly income for the time being exceeds the following scale .—One person in the family Iss. per week, two persons in the family 18s. per week, three persons in the family 21s. per week, and so on; 3s. for each additional member of the family. Amendment. —No person shall be admitted as an nut- 'patient who is in receipt of parochial relief, or whose weekly income exceeds the following scale: —Single man or woman 175., married couple 205.,f0r each child ls.6d. Any case of accident will be attended to in the first in. stance without recommendation, but, when attending the second time, the patient is required to bring a form of recommendation fully and properly filled up, the House Visitors and Finance Committee to have power to deal with special and exceptional cases." Mr. Mosss said he did not know how it came about that the scale was raised for single and married people and reduced for children. Everybody who had had the care of a family knew that they could not keep a child for is. 6d. per week. He did not see any reason for alter- ing the scale as regarded children. It would be a hard- ship upon those who had children because they reduced the scale. With the old rule, a man who earned 245. per week, and had two children, was admitted to the benefits of the institution, but by the new rule only a man who had 235. a week could enjoy it. Practically they fined:a man who was most deserving of their con- sideration. He would move that it should read ;—" for each child 3s. '• instead of " for each child is. 6d. Mr. JOHN ROBERTS said he had great pleasure in seconding the proposition. Dr. Fox said the rules were requested to be altered and a committee was formed consisting of twc members of the medical profession, two members of the working- men's committee and three of the Board of Manage- ment, and after many discussions, the rule was agreed to as amended. If Mr. Monks's amendment was car- ried, commencing with 17s. a week it would bring it up to 355., for a man with five children, that he took to be the average family in VVarriegton. The rules were submitted to the Board of Management and they approved of them. They were also submitted to and approved by the malical staff and the working- men's executive. Mr. ROBERTS said they were not approved by the Workingmen's Committee. Dr. Fox gave the scale and contributions of the Pres- ton, Wigan,and Manchester Royal Infirmaries. He sub- initted that if Mr. Monks's scale was adopted,they would include half the population of Warrington and the work certainly could not be carried out by the house surgeon. They would have to increase the staff to four if the peo- ple took full advantage of the privilege and instead of arrears in the income ot £4O, they would probably have arrears of £4OO, which meant either insolvency or nefficiency. Lastly he would say that if that scale was adopted, the subscriptions would be less, because people would say they will heln people who are able to help themselves and who are not objects of charity." He hoped in the interests of the institution that Mr. Monks's amendment would not be carried. _ _

Mr. THOMPSON said that at the first meeting of the comreittee for revising the rules, there was no dissent whatever, but there was at a fuither meeting. When he proposed the alteration of the rule it was with a sincere attempt to benefit the workingmen of Warring- ton. Mr. Roberts was on the committee and then was the proper time to have Epoken against the alteration of the rules. He (Mr. Thompson) never heard him speak at he meetings. He would prefer going back to the old rule.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL 4, 186

Mr. reyLon said that on behalf of the workingmen's comniittre, they were of e,pinion that the old rule had better reain. After furtuer discussion Mr. TnomPsoN moved and Dr. nicumorin sec)nded, that the old rule be continued, with the adoption of the foot note.

The motion was carried. The other amendments and the rules were carried. HOUSE SURGEON'S REPORT. Dr. LAN DALE read the senior house surgeon's report. —Mr. PARR s,id the report showed that the benefits of the institution had been widely taken advantage of. Be moved that the report be adopted and the senior house surgeon thank-a for his comprehensive report. TREASURER'S REPORT.

The TREASURER. (Mr, G. Dakin), read the statement of accounts for the year, which showed that the income for the year 1895 up tu Decemher, was £649 ss. 2d. against £647 4s. Bd. the previous year. The annual subscriptions were £329 93., against £321 6.1. Donations £232 us. 61 against £53 as. They got 100 guineas from the Amateur Dramatic Society and an anonymous donation of £6O. The Church collections were £212 7s. 2c; , being an increase of £l9 us. 4d. over last year. The Workingmen's Committee had subscribed £522 is. 3d., against £442 18s. id. Patients pence, £ll2 Os. 3d.. against £74 193. 9d. Proceeds of annual ball, £95 ss. 3d., against £35 18s, 2d. Contributions by in-paying patients £1 195., against £9 7s. ld.The working income was -.22,207 18s. 5d an increase of £396 6s. Bd. ELECTION OF HONORARY MEDICAL OFFICERS.

The following were re-elected as honorary medical officers :—Drs. White, Smith, Gornall. Fox, Joseph and Richmoni.—Mr. Parr suggested that the name of Dr. Bowden be added to the list.—Dr. Gornall said he had been connected with the institution for 30 years, and there had generally been candidates for any position that was vacant. He thought the position ought to be de- clared vacant,and all who were in practice at Warring- ton ought to have the opportunity of being candidates for any position not connected with the institu- tion. He did not object to Dr. Bowden, but he thought it was an unusual thing that an appointment should be filled in such a manner.— Mr. Parr said he did not feel inclined to alter it. When the suggestion was first made Dr. Gornall should have objected, that would have been the proper time.—Dr. Gornall said he neverheard the suggestion.—Mr. Thomp- son said it was hardly fair to gentlemen practi ing for a number of years, that Dr. Bowden should be put on in that way.—Mr. Parr said he was reminded that it was informal, but if the meeting was desirous,he was willing that the proposal be put over to the next meeting.—Dr. Richmond said he and Dr. Joseph were added to the list in the same way.— Mr. Monks said he thought they had had friction enough during the last twelve months and ho hoped nothing would be said to increase it.—Mr. Dakin proposed that Dr. Bowden be elected an honorary medical officer.—Mr. Taylor seconded.—Mr. Monks moved that no addition be made to the list of honorary medical officers without notice.—Mr. Thompson seconded.—The amendmmt was put first and carried. TAKING COLD.

This is the time of year for colds. Notwithstanding the fact that everybody knows the serious effects which may result from taking cold, I venture to say there are millions of careless people in Great Britain who are more or less in danger. Colds are the beginning, or at least the outside signs of bronchitis, consumption, influenza; in fact complications at this time may result in anything, even to death. If you take cold easily, you are weak. If you are suffering from the effects of taking cold, you are in still greater danger. A specific which merely relieves the local affection of a cold is not enough for this climate. The system must be so fortified that it can resist the climate. Colds then will not be so serious.

The case of a little girl who nearly died of complica- tions following a severe cold, is told in a letter which reads as follows : " I take great pleasure in adding my testimonial to the hundreds of others who speak so highly of Scott's Emulsion as a remedial agent. We had occasion to give the Emulsion to our little girl, age five years, who had been ill six months with a complication of ailments, which started with a severe cold. Her cough became chronic, and developed into ghooplng cough. She then had measles and finally bronchitis set in, BO that you may imagine how emaciated and weak she was when we began giving her Scott's Emulsion. After she had the first bottle she began to improve rapidly, and after , five bottles were taken she had completely recovered. She gained flesh and strength, and the local irritation of the throat was not only relieved, but speedily cured. She is now in good health, and, in fact, is quite robust. We lvere very much in doubt at one time about raising the c'hild, especially when she had bronchit urs very truly, (signed), FREDERICK GEORGE RYDER, 55, John-street, Ordnance Place, Chatham, December 18th, 1895. . .

, Scott's Emulsion is a remedy which combines nourish- ment with healing and curative properties. It increases vitality, makes flesh, vitalizes the blood, allays irrita- tion of throat or lungs, and gives nourishment to the whole system. If taken for colds and chronic coughing it prevents complications, such as bronchitis or pneu- monia, by giving the system strength to recover from its weakened state, and soon restores health. Scott's Emulsion is daily effecting wonderful cures of affections of the breathing organs. Hundreds of letters testify to this. The only genuine Scott's Emulsion has a trade- mark of fisherman with a big fish on his) back, on the wrapper.

GROSS CRUELTY TO A DAUGHTER AT

WARRINGTON.

A FATHER COMMIrTED FOR THREE MONTHS' HARD LABOUR.

Frederick Roberts, lodging at 44, Aiken-st., was sum- moned at the Warrington Borough Court, on Friday, be- fore Dr. Gornall (chairman), Councillor Roberts,and Mr. S. Wright, on the charge of neglecting three children on March 10th, and divers other dates within the last six months.—lnspector McAteer' N.S.P.C.C., said that the defendant was charged with neglecting three children, Hannah, aged 15 years ; Frederick,six years; and John, three years. In consequence of a complaint made to him, on March 10th, he went to the house of defendant and there found the two youngest children in the most deplorable state of filth and almest naked. There was scarcely anything in the house which was also in a dirty condition. The girl, 15 years of age, whom he was charged with neglecting was almost an imbecile and had lost all power in her right arm, and right side. This girl would tell them that on the night of the 6th inst., she was turned out of the house at 10.30 when it was very wet and she was taken in by a neighbour named Mrs. Benison, of 39, Aiken-street, who informed P.C. Stuart. When examined, it was found that she was dressed in one garment and a chemise which had not been washed for a long time. He himself had never seen a girl so filthy. Vermin was all over her body and the smell was intolerable. Her body was eaten in with the vermin and her clothes were so bad that Mrs. Harrison had to throw them into the back yard. The girl had remained up to the present time at Mrs. Harrison's house, and although the defendant knew that she was there, he never went to see her at all. He (the inspector) had repeatedly warned defendant, and he had also ben brought before the magistrates on the same charge.—The defendant was then asked if he had any questions to ask the inspector, and he began in a violent manner to deny the charge, and lifted up one of his children who was in court and banged it upon the solicitor's table asking the magistrates if they could see any cruelty done to them. The police at once prevented him from continuing in this manner, and P.C. Stuart gave evidence to the scandalous condition in which the girl Hannah was when taken in by Mrs. Harrison. He said that the other children were also in a similar con- dition.

Mrs. Harrison, 39, Aiken-st., said that on the 6th of the present month, at about 10.30 p.m., she saw the girl Hannah in the street, although it was pouring with rain. She took her into her house and upon examining her she found her in the condition that the Inspector had des- cribed. The clothes which were thrown into the yard, were" almost walking," and her husband was not well yet from the smell of them.—Similar evidence was given by Mrs. Dawson, Mrs. Waterfield,and P.C. Evans.—The defendant denied using any cruelty to his children.—Dr. Gornall in giving the decision of the Bench, said that personally he hardly dared to say what he thought of the case. He had known the defendant for several years and he had asked his brother magistrates whether he ought to be allowed to sit on the Bench during the hear- ing of the case, as he knew defendant was guilty as soon as he entered the box. Three years ago, the de- fendant was in the same state of filth as now,and he had been so ever since, and he was the cause of an outbreak of fever in Aiken-st. That fever was communicated to others than his own family,and he (Dr. Gornall)thought he ought to have been punished for that. The defendant had been the cause of a nurse in the hospital taking the fever, and they nearly lost her. He would say no more, but would give the judgment of the court which de- fendant richly deserved, and that was that he would be sent for three months' imprisonment with the hardest labour it was possible to give.

===== 0002601_18960404_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
USE OF BLOODHOUNDS BY THE • POLICE.

The " Field " publishes the following account of a case in which a bloodhound was used by a country con- stable to track poachers, stating that the truth of the officer's narrative is vouched for by the police superin- tendent and the breeder of the dog :—" Some time this year a constable was out in the early morning, when about 0.30 a.m. he came across a couple of notorious poachers who were walking along a footpath through some fields. They, seeing the constable, called out in alarm as a signal to their companions, who were no doubt coming behind. Owing to the darkness the latter escaped ; but the constable took some rabbits and nets from the men he had met, for being in the possession of which under such circumstances they were punished Later. At daybreak the constable, accompanied by a young bloodhound bitch, returned to the place, and was able to distinguish the footsteps of a number of men who had come out of a turnip field. They had separated, some going in one direction, others in another. The hotted was put upon the tracks, and with her nose to the ground she hunted them across two fields,going straight up to sundry bags of game which had been hidden in a hedgerow. So far, so good ; but the constable was not yet satisfied, and he took his hound back to where she had originally been laid on the line. This time she went off ha another direction, and soon left the policeman far behind. He following up, however, ultimately found her standing at another hedgerow, where more bags of game were found concealed. They were secretly watched all day, but the poachers must have smelled a rat, for none of themselves or their families came near. At night the constable and the lessee of the shooting concealed themselves near the place where the first lot of game was discovered. Now they had not long to wait, for in about half-an-hour there came a sound of approaching footsteps, and two men appeared, who immediately appropriated the bags and their contents,which included the nets and the usual poacher's paraphernalia. They were at once recognised, and, the spoil taken from them, were allowed to go. Summonses followed in due course, and when the case was heard a plea was sent up that they had not taken the game themselves, but had been sent for it by their mates. Fives of 40s. and costs were imposed, or, failing the payment, a month's imprisonment." On the general que3t inn of the use of bloodhounds by the police, the " Field" says :—" Bloodhounds or hounds of any other kind would be simply of no avail as detectives in thoroughfares and other places where passers by are fre- quent ; but in country districts it is a different matter altogether. No doubt, too,a bloodhound trained for the purpose might be of considerable use to the tur 11 con- stabulary in the detection of poachers and criminals, who otherwise might escape justice. Still the employ- ment of dogs for such a purpo,o will probably meet with strong opposition until it is satisfactorily understood that whet, the hound has brought his human quarry 'to hay,' bi- will not worry and eat km. As a fact, the blood- hound is not such a cruel and savage beast as his name implies ; and when he has found the man whose foot- steps he has carefully scented out, be is content to bay or lark at him until assistai.ce comes. The modern .11.)und never attempts to make a meal of his game.

SHIP CANAL.

LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

Last week's seagoing traffic upon the Ship Canal was below the average; it showed a falling off of 39 ships and 18,489 tons as compared with the preceding seven days. The arrivals included fourteen vessels with general cargoes thirteen with stone and slate, twelve with pig iron, three with wood pulp, two with china clay, and one each with cotton timber, logwood and green fruit. Twenty vessels sailed with general car:oes, two with pitch, two with salt, and two with coal. The following is an approximate statement of the traffic for the week ended March 27 ;—lnwards: Coastwise, ships 43. net tonnage 6,783 ; foreign, ships 12, net tonnage 6,947 ; total, ships 55, net tonnage 13,730. Outwards: Coastwise, ships 46, net tonnage 8,498 ; foreign, ships 16, net tonnage 8,922 ; total,ships 62, net tonnage 17,420 Total inwards and. outwards : Ships 117, net tonnage 31,150.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

The large audience pt the meeting of this society on Sunday, despite the fine weather and other attractions, testified to the hold the society has gained on a great number of men in the town. Bv.,"WhoBo breaketh an hedge,a serpent shall bite him." Wise men, he said, made hedges,but fools broke through them,and he suggested various safeguards which his hearers might adopt to secure themselves and families against temptations. Mr. W. H. Payton skilfully performed an organ solo. Messrs .Thomas Wallingtonl(secretary),John Atkinson, and Haley also assisted in the. service.
