"THE FUTURE OF LABOUR."

LECTURE BY MR. J. WILSON, M.P.

There was a eur attendance of both local and War- rington peeple at the Wesleyen Church.Lymm, on Mon- day evening, when Mr. J Wilson,M.P.,for Mid-Durham, delivered an address on " fhe Future of Labour." Mr. John Rylands, J P., occupied the char, and was well received. Be was supported by Councillor H. Roberts, J.P. (Warrington), Mr. Thomas Morris, F.G.S. (Warr- ington), and Mr. J. Dodds. The Chairman in referring to politics, said he did not know whethPr they read the debates in the House of Core- Lords or not. Tf they did,and if that did not cure them of any idea of Toryism he did not know what would. He thought it had rather cured him. The thing was to get hold of the right principles and stick to t em under all circumstances. They should not be tempted to get rid of them. He did not think the Lymm people would. They had tried to return Liberals in the district which was generally anything but Liberal, and he was sorry to say that they failed. He had done a little to help them but he regretted th •t his anxious wish to achieve some- thing had failed. They had had a few good candidates but they had been unable to return them. They had no candidate at present before them and he was afraid it would be some time before they could get one. It was not an encouragement for an ordinary man to come there as a candidate when some extraordinary man had failed. Alluding to the proposals to do away with the death duties Mr. Rylands hoped the Government would find that it was impossible to abolish those duties. Mr. Wilson said that for the last 30 years he had been in either a secondary or primary positien in Trades' Union organisations, and for many years becore in both England and America he had tried to take his part. He believed there were great mistakes made in Many direc- tions as to what was included in labour. If they took the working class of this country and saw the various organisations and movements that were exis'ing they would find a very clear cleavage and dividing line separating them into two distinct schools. Some men claimed that there was no labour except what was performed by the hand. He himself was fully aware of the importance of manual labour, but he was as fully aware and as ready to confess that a man could work with his brain as well as he could with his hand. (Hear, hear and applause). The wealth of this country was as much attributa.ble to man's brain as to his hand. (Applause.) Wherever a , man was wasting his energies, that man whether he I was high or low, rich or poor.was in his opinion outside the term and definition and meaning of the word •'labour." (Flear,hear.) The two schools could be defined as optimistic and pessimistic. Those men who were full of hope and recognised the great change that the world had made were of the former, aud the men who said there had been no change, and whose talk was gloomy, sad and sombre to a great extent, were of the latter. He himself was one of the optimists. He re- cognised that we had made a great change, and he was living in hope that in the few years remaining of his life the working class would make greater changes than they had made. (Hear, hear.) Wherever there was the greatest self-reliance amongst the working, people there was the greatest advance to be found. (Hear,hear. and applause.) Another class denounced those who had money. He did not. He recognised that rich men often had feelings of generosity, and it ill became them to generally denounce everybody with more money than themselves. But he would denounce any man whatever who did not act the part of a man, and try to bring about the brotherhood of men and the establishment of concord. (Applause.) To the questions "Have we made any advance ?" "Is there any change ?" he needed no other answer than his own life, and he needed no other experience than his own within the compass of his life in the mines of this country. He could recognise the blissful changes that had come over the occupation in which he worked. He had worked down the mines as a lad for 11. hours a day, week in week out. He had worked underground so that he never saw day- light except on Sunday,and then he was often too sleepy to look at it. (Laughter.) There was now no coal- getter in the mines of Durham who could be down above , 7 hours a day, to-day. Thera had also a change come over the care for human life he was thankful to say. One of the greatest mysteries in human life to him was a Conservative representing, a district where the working- man had the power to put in a Liberal. (Hear, hear.) The ignorance of the workingmen was fast dying out. It used to be as true as that the day was light that, as Lord Beaconsfield said, the Queen reigned over two nations separate and distinct. OF late they saw that bishops had been specially appointed to represent the working-class, but he did not know why that duty had been relegated to the clerical shoulders. He did not think any man in the country need fear the advance of the working-class. (Hear, hear.) They loved justice too well to be unjust to other people. (Applause.) No nation could be born in a day and the wounds and sore places of our industrial life could'not be corrected by any act of parliament whatsoever. They aimed at brother- hood but it was no mere meaningless mouthing but a reality which they believed would be attained. (Applause.) Referring to the increase of the navy, Mr. Wilson con- tended that what this country wanted was to be at peace with all men. (Hear, hear.) We were an indus- trial nation and one of the deadliest enemies of this country was the spirit of war and the uncertainty of war. The war spirit had lately done a lot to damp down the signs of improvement. If we built a fleet to-day it would be out of fashion next year. The vessels were like the ladies' bonnets. (Laughter.) Hewes in favour of short hours of labour but against them being pro- cured by state aid. (Applause.) Another thing that he advocated was better homes for the people. (Hear, hear.) The vile slums in some of our cities'were objects for a national work. The true wealth of a nation was the health of the people, morally, mentally, and physi- cally. (Applause.) In conclusion Mr. Wilson said he believed that there should be a large contingent of labour representatives in the House of Commons but'he was not going to damage the Liberals, and let the Con- servatives have full power. (Hear, hear and applause.) Councillor Roberts moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Wilson for his able and instructive address. He said this was the first occasion he had made a public appear- ance in Lymm for 20 odd years. He had Senile experi- ence in his time of workingmen,as for some years he had had the honour to be one of the representatives of one of the constituencies of Warrington in that important assembly known as the Town Council. He could fully endorse everything Mr. Wilson had said,in regard to the alteration of tone-and of opinion among the more intelli- gent constituents of his ward in Warrington.When he re- membered that the past of labour bad been such a won- derful past he had no doubt as to its future. He had heard his father say that in his early days the working- man's foodPwae taxed,his light was taxed,and his very life wastaxed;his knowledge was taxed ,and the right of pub- lic meetings was a thing unknown and there were men living who remembered that when workingmen met in Manchester for the purpose of discussing in public their grievances they were mercilessly cut down by the soldiers. The young men of to-day, unfortunately for themselves did not realize the sacrifices,which theiefathers had made that they might enjoy the privileges and advantages of to-day. When he remembered too that it was the noble spirit of:patriotism and self-sacrifice which had caused the alteration of the conditions of the working classes of England, and that that spirit remained to day among the descendants of those men,he had no doubt whatever that the future of the workingman would be grander,greater, and brighter that those who had passed away a genera- tion ago ever conceived. The speaker supposed that the spirit of socialism so absurdly propounded by the I.L.P. OM not obtain to the extent that its frothy publications would have them believe. In addition and above all, he believed and thought that the success of the future de- pended and would depend on the protection of the Chris- tian faith in which we believed. The fact was that men were becoming more Christian. (Applause.) Mr. Thomas Morris seconded the proposition, and said that Mr. Wilson to his mind was the type of an honest working-man. As such his sentences had proved him to be. He added that unless a man worked for his liv- ing he ought not to eat. If he would not work and lived, some of them had to support him. The vote was carried unanimously. Mr. Wilson proposed a vote of thanks to the Chair- man, and Mr. Dodds in seconding, commented upon the self-sacrifice entailed upon such an elderly man as Mr. Rylands by his coming out, to preside for them. This resolution was likewise heartily accorded and the meeting concluded.

Dr. Stocks, of Rankeillor-st., Edinbutgh, who was arrested-a fortnight ago on a charge of performing an illegal operation. and who was remitted from the police court on a charge of murder,has been liberated on £2OO bail. The Crown authorities, it is said, have modified the charge to one of culpable homicide. In 1894 Germany supplied this country with thirteen m illion nine hundred and ninety-eight thousand eight hundred and ninety-two hundredweight of sugar,valued at nine million six hundred and sixty-five thousand six hundred and seventy-five pounds. The list of honours for officers engaged in the recent walk to Coomassie and back is published. It is of about the same proportions as a "Gazette " would have been after an important engagement in the Peninsula or the Crimea.

The revenue returns for the financial year were made up and published on Tuesday night. Tho total amount paid into the Exchequer was £109,339,946, being an in crease on the preceding year of no less than £7,642,642.

===== 0002601_18960404_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
Y.M.C.A. CHORAL SOCIETY.

This society which is conducted by the Rev.E. Moore, G.T.S.C., gave a capital reneition of Gaul's cantata " Una " in the Parr Hall, Warrington, on Monday even- ing. The story of Una is adapted from Spencer's "Fairie Queen," and in the hands of Gaul who composed the cantata for the Norwich Musical Festival,so recently as 1893,it has received a musicianly touch which should make it one of the most popular of cantatas. Its per- formance on Monday lasted about two hours, and the remainder of the evening was occupied by Mr. Jude,of Liverpool, who gave a short organ recital and also sang a solo. There was but a very small attendance at what proved a most interesting concert, and it is questionable whether the Y.M.C.A. funds will be at all benefited. The chorus was not a very strong one—not more than fifty voices—and lacked a little assistance in the bass to make it well balanced. Nevertheless, the difficult and exacting music of the composition was rendered in a manner which gained the well-merited approval of the audience. The pieces deserving special mention were the opening chorus" Hearken! 0 Sylvanus, hearken !"; the chorus of peasant labourers, " Let's hasten on" ; arid the concluding chorus—a really fine ne—" Hymen 16 Hymen !" They were given with a power and finish; light and shade were not neglected, and the volume of sound was full and sonorous. The four leading vocalists were Una. Miss Eleanor Birkett (soprano) ; Fidessa—an enchantress, Miss Letty Field (contralto) ; the Redcrosse Knight, Mr. Joseph M. Bate (tenor) ; and the hermit—a magician, and the knight, Prince Arthur, Mr. P. 1. Bate (bass). The two male singers are Warringtonians but Miss Birkett hailed from Stockport, and Miss Field from Birkenhead. In the cantata there are solos for each of the four voices. Miss Birkett created a very favourable impression. Gifted with a beautiful soprano voice she sang with artistic culture, clear enuncia- tion, and admirable expression, and it was evid- ent that the audience would be glad to renew ' her acquaintance on some future occasion. In the song, "0 love will love," she was particularly fine, and in response to continued applause she bowed her acknow- ledgments. Miss Field was not so successful in the recit alloted to her, but she was heard to greater advan- tage in the concerted music. Mr. Joseph Bate used his pare-toned tenor voice with creditable effect, giving a pleasing rendition of his parts. His brother, Mr. P. I. Bate, sang best in the song of Prince Arthur, but we are not quite sure whether he has not been heard to better advantage on previous occasions. Of the concerted music, a very pleasing number was the quartet for the four leading voices "Thou whom I love—l love so well," and the succeeding duet taken part in by Redcrosse and Una was also very good—the blending of voices being all that could be desired. Mention mus'; also 'be made of the performance of the orchestral band, the accompaniments being played in a manner that heightened the effectiveness of the choral work,and added much to the enjoyment of the audience. The overture and the instrumental intermezzo were also well rendered. MilS Toft fulfilled the duties of pianist and due credit must be accorded to the Rev. E. Moore, the conductor, who wielded the baton with his accustomed skill, piloting all through many difficulties. It is to be hoped that his concert will become an annual one, and that in future he may receive greater public support. The name of Mr. Jude is so well-known, and his abilities as organist and musical composer so much appreciated in Warrington, that it is hardly necessary here to dilate upon them. He commenced a short programme with an organ solo of his own composition, descriptive of a storm, which he depicted in such a manner as to render it very realistic. For this masterly performance he was awarded an encore. " The man of sorrows" was a song very appropriate for this time of the year, and Mr. Jude gave such a rendering of it as made apparent its solid intrinsic worth. The concluding item was G-uilmant's "Marche Funebre et Chant Seraphique " which the audience greatly appreciated.

THE ALLEGED WARRINGTON BIGAMY

CASE.

STRANGE STATEMENTS. "TRUTH STRANGER THAN FICTION."

On Monday morning befer.P the Mayor (chairman), and Alderman Harrison, Harriet Annie Ellis, or_Gaddes, was charged with having wilfully and unlawfully on the 18th November, 1894,married Charles Lewis Geddes, at the Runcorn Parish Church, her farmer husband being then alive.

Mr. A. Browne prosecuted, and Mr. P. Davies do. ended.

Mr. Browne said that his client Geddes was an engine tenter and had been for 23 years in one employment. The warrant for prisoner's arrest was executed by Detec- tive-Sergeant Monaghan at Ellesmere Port. Mr. Browne said he did not think there could be any defence as he had both marriage lines and witnesses of each form of marriage. There could be no other ending to the case except to commit the prisoner to the Assizes for bigamy. His client desired to say publicly that he would adopt prisoner's child whenever it was old enough to leave its mother.

Charles Lewis Geddes, engine tenter, 43 Gladstone-st., Warrington, said he first became acquainted with pris- oner about three years ago and courted her. He eventu- ally put up the banns of marriage and went through the form of marriage with her in Runcorn Parish Church on the 18th November, 1894. Prisoner said she was a widow and he subsequently lived with her in two different houses in Warrington. They were happy and comforta- ble. On the 12th March, 1896,5 he left his house leaving word with his daughter where she was going. She did not return and on Saturday the 15th March he discovered a number of articles were missing and subsequently that a number were pawned. He applied for a warrant against prisoner for larceny. In cross-examination by Mr. Davies: If the first mar- riage turned out invalid he would not take her back, as she left him. He did not know before she went that her husband had returned. He afterwards found that his wife did not go on her own accopnt but was brought from his house. Before his marriage he knew her about 12 months. She told him th t she had been married be- fore but her husband was dead. He was easily deceived. She said her husband died in hospital. She never told him he was a lad of 15. Further she never said she saw her husband die, but that he died in hospital of typhoid fever. She did not say that she thought he was dead. She told him that he was a scoundrel. By Mr. Browne : He would not have married her if she had said she only thought her husband was dead. Edward Price said he was clerk at the Parish Church of Stoak. He was present when the marriage took place on Bth April, 1889, when the prisoner then Harriet Wright, was marriel to Charles Henry Ellis. He saw Charles Henry Ellisin court. He made his mark as a witness in the register. They were married by the vicar, the Rev. C. B. Griffiths. His (witness's) son Enoch was also present. On the certificate Ellis's age was given as 18. He looked boyish. Prisoner also gave her age as 18.

Sergeant Mcnaghan produced the warrant far larceny a2alnst prisoner. Prisoner was apprehended by him at Ellesmere Port on the 23rd March. He brought her to Warrington, and she was in custody in Warrington Police Station on Tuesday last. On that day he charged her with committing bigamy, and she made no reply. He subsequently went to the Parish Church of Stoak. Previous to that he received from Charles Henry Ellis, husband of defendant, the in+ Triage certificate marked "A," which he now produce(l. He then examined the certificate in Stoak Parish Church and found it to corres- pond with the register. He also produced a certificate of marriage marked " B" between Geddes and prisoner, dated 18th November, 1894. He had since been to Run- corn Parish Church and found it correct. When she was apprehended she was in company with her husband in the street.

Mr. P. Davies said that it was very true that truth was stranger than fiction and he thought this was a case in point. Be wished to show their worships that this was a case in which there was no prima facie evidence, and that no jury would convict the prisoner upon it. They had the power to listen to this evidence on her behalf and the witnesses which she might call. There was no case of bigamy whatever`to be charged against her. The law by Stone as to invalid marriage was that in the publication of banns any misdescription must be with the knowledge of both parties. A valid marriage must be proved in a bigamy case. The law would not presume ; it in any case of bigamy—it must be proved that it was la valid marriage. If the banns were published in the wrong name in the knowledge of both parties for the purpose of clandestine marriage, the marriage was void. He relied upon that. They bad to satisfy themselves that the one produced was a true marriage certificate of a true marriage between two persons. He was going to show them that from beginning to end it was false. To carry out his clandestine intention that young soldier (prisoner's first husband) had the entire knowledge of the girl. Fortunately she could not la. charged with bigamy as the marriage with the first husband was in- valid. Mr. Davies went on to say that Ellis was at that time a lad of 15 working on the Ship Canal. He fell in love with the prisoner who was about 17. His father was a farmer living in the district of Ellesmere Port and his name was Ellis. Charles Henry Ellis's name was not Ellis but Lloyd—Charles Henry Lloyd. ne fell in love with, not Harriet Wright, (the name pri- soner was married under) but with Harriet Wilkinson. They got married under false names. They married as Charles Henry Ellis and Harriet Wright. Ellis said he was 18—he was only 15. His putative father, David Ellis, he said was dead and be was alive to-Jay. He wished to keep it from his father. Ellis's name was Lloyd as he was the son of Mrs. Ellis before wedlcek. The prisoner's name was not Wright but Wilkinson, and she was not 18 years of are but only 17. She said her father's name was Thomas Wright whereas it was Thomas Wilkinson. If the banns had been published in only one wrong name the marriage was false. But both were wrong. Mr. Browne :It is evidently a scandalous attempt to get out of having the woman as a wife. It is making her a catspaw. Mr. Davies: You are talking in a very strong manner about a lad will was only 15. Ha never lived ‘-via h her but left the place the day he was married. Mr. Browne (looking at the certificate) : Her mothe'

was actually a witness of the marriage. Mr. Davies, continuing, said Lloyd was thought to be dying of typhoid fever in hospital in 1891. He said to prisoner "You can go and do what you like; our marriage is illhgal as we were married in the wrong names." She thought he was dying, and never saw him from that day until he came from abroad. After his ill- ness Lloyd workQu at Acton Grange, Bootle, Wrexham, and then enlisted at Chester and was sent to York and Aldershot. In 1894 he was sent with his regiment to Rangoon in Burmah and when returning the regiment was stopped at Gibraltar and sent away with the Athanti expedition. tie only ietirned to England in February last. Once he had written to her from Bur- mah but received no reply. Mr. Browne : He din receive a letter in reply. He received a reply in June 1894. Prisoner was then in Warrington as a domestic servant.

Mr. Davies said that Llpsd on his return made en- quiries as he wanted prisoner back again. When he dis- covered where she was he fetched her. She had n: money end she wrongfully p.iwned some of Geddes' goods, in- tending, however, to pay him back again. He thought that they would agree with him that the marriage .was invalid._ _

The magistrates considered there was a prima facie case, and committed prisoner to the Liverpool Assiz3s. Bail was allowed in prisoner's own recaanizances._ _

During the hearing of the case it was remarked that this was the first bigamy case in the borough of Mar- rington for 30 years.

MUTINOUS SEAMEN ON TEIE SHIP CANAL.

SEVERE SENTENCE AT DARESBURY SESSIONS. On Tuesday morning at D tresbury Petty Sessions,Mr. W. Long in the chair, two seam n named John Murphy and Patrick Dwyer who had ben engaged on the steamer Wooler were charged with disobeying the com- mands of Arthur Samuel Cole, the master, also with as- saulting him,and further with assaulting the second offi- cer named Hitehen,on the Manchester Ship Canal on the 19th inst. Murphy ileaded guilty to assaulting lichen, but Dwyer denied all the charges. Mr. Cobbett.. of Cobbett and Cobbett, solicitors, Manchester. prosecuted. In opening the case Mr. Conbetr, said the proceedings against the prisoners were taken under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1891, section 225, dealing with offences against disc'pline. The offence of wilful disobedience rendered the prisoners liable to imprisonment not ex- ceeding four weeks, and to forfeit out of tneir wages a sum not exceeding two days' pay. For the assault they were liable for imprisonment not exceeding twelve weeks. The men were seamen on board the steamship Wooler, under an engagement to proceed to Teneriffe and other places and back again to Manchester, and then to Newport in M minouthshire. The vessel had made her voyage back to Manchester and was proceed- ing down the canal on her way to Newort. The two prisoners were absent when the ship sailed from Manchester on the 19th of the present month, and were duly marked in the log as being absent. When the steamer reached Mode Wheel locks, the two prisoners were on the quay. Both of them seemed more or less under the influence of drink, and they attempted to get on board. Murphy succeeded, but Dwyer fell into the lock from which he was rescued. When they were both on board, although all the attention of the captain and pilot was nrcessiry to get the ship safely through the lock, Dwyer went up the bridge and demanded to be paid off at once. The captain refused, and told him to go below, but Diyyer lifted up his hand and threatened to strike the captain. Assistance was got, and after a struggle he was put in irons. For a short time things were quiet, but subsequently Murphy freed the prisoner.* Dwyer from his irons and the two attacked Hitchen. The captain, who Wats. on the bridge,hearing the cries for help, came down and found that the two prisoners were doing their best to throw the mate overboard, and they were threatening to put an end to him. After a severe, straggle, they were overpowered and put in irons, and given-in custody at Latchford. All the way to this point, the prisoners behaved in a most violent and disorderly manner. They had to be fastened to the masts to keep them quiet. Affairs of that kind on board ship were of great consequence, because P t the time the disturbance happened, the ship was on the canal_ The pilot and captain had to give every attention to what they were doing and to see that no accident happened. The owners asked that the prisoners should be sent to prison for a term so as to be a warning to them a•;.cl others. Captain Cole of the steamer Wooler, corroborated Mr. Cobbett's statement. Dwyer came to him on the bridge, and stated that he would not go until -be had his life. Both prisoners were under the influence of drink,and he had to place them in irons.—William James Huntingdon, pilot,of Seacornbe, and Henry Edward Elitchens, second officer on the steamer, also gave evidence for the prose, cution. The latter stated that when Murphy had un- loosed Dwyer from the irons,both of them attacked him, and endeavoured to throw hint overboard. His body was overhanging the side cf the vessel, and had the pri- soners succeeded in unloosing his legs from a stanchion he would have fallen overboard.—Patrick Brunt spoke to going to the assistance of Hitchen.—Murphy in his defence,said both Dwyer and himself were subject to as- saults,and comp!ained of brutal treatment at the bands of the officers.—Dwyer said he was under the influence of drink at the time and he did not know exactly what he did,or how he got into that trouble. He had got good discharges from other vessels. The magistrates considered the case fully proved, and the chairman said that even as landsmen they could see the fearful consequences which might attend such con- duct. They would be sent to prison for one month on each count, three months each in all.

GRAP.EIIC ACCOUNT OF A VISIT TO ONE

MCDOUGALL'S PATENT SELFRAISING FLOUR ONCE TRIMIs usBD. THE EXAMINER SATURPAY, APRIL 4. 1896.

===== 0002601_18960404_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXA_MINER.

SATURDAY, APRIL 4tb, 1896.

THERE can be no question that The New Education Bill. the Education Bill brought into the House of Commons on Tues- day by Sir JOHN GORST, on behalf of the Govern- ment, will make a complete revolution in the educational system of the country. Until the full text of the Bill has been printed and studied it is not possible to enter into a detailed examination of the measure, but one important point was made perfectly clear by Sir JOHN GORST.S speech, namely, that the most reactionary members of the I Government have had the chief part ir shaping this new departure. There are, as everyone knows, men in the Cabinet who were formerly Liberals and who still deny that they are Tories, but either these Liberal Unionists have had very little to do with th e new Bil or they have managed to swallow in a very complete fashion some of the principles which they at one time most stoutly maintained. The Bill, taken as a whole, is an insidious and deliberate attempt to deprive the people of the country of a complete and direct control of their educational artem. Sir JOHN GORST had a good deal to say about the importance of the work carried on by the voluntary schools, but all this was really beside the mark. No one wishes to dispute the fact that education is pro- vided for some half-million more children in volun- tary schools than in Board schools, or that many millions of pounds would have to be expended in order to take over the voluntary schools. The benefits that have been derived, financially or otherwise, from the voluntary school have been amply acknowledged, and very little objection has been raised in any quarter to the suggestion that additional grants should be made from the Imperial Exchequer in aid of these schools. What has been demanded by education reformers is that additional public grants should be accompanied by additional public control, and also that no at- tempt should be made to restrict or fetter those schools which are already under the management of popularly-elected School Boards. In the pre- sent Bill neither of these conditions has been com- plied with. A special grant of four shillings per head for every child in voluntary schools is to be made, amounting in all it is said to half a million pounds for England and Wales, but no further public control of the schools is provided for. On the other hand School Boards, even in the largest towns, such as London and Birming- ham and Manchester, will be checked and re- stricted in the most unwarrantable, and it might almost be said,:insulting manner. It is proposed that in the future in all counties and boroughs a new educational.,authority, shall be created—" a paramount authority, the one channel through which public money is to :arrive at the different schools." Each County Council and Town Coun- cil will, it appears, be required to form an Educa- tion Committee and through these Committees all grants will be made to the voluntary schools or to the necessitous Board schools. On the face of it the arrangement would appear to provide for at least an indirect public control, but that is evi- dently not the intention of the Government. These Committees will men:lly be the mediums through which the grants will be conveyed to the schools but the schools will still remain entirely in the hands of the present managers. The schools may, if it is desired, combine together according to districts or denominations and divide the grant that is due to them in their own way amongst themselves, subject to the approval of the Education Department. The setting up of another educational anthority in the shape of committees appears to be an extremely cumbrous way of distributing grants in aid of education, but the real object of doing so is very readily seen in other provisions of the new Bill. The com- mittees will not only distribute these grants, but they will also hamper the School Boards and the Board schools, and they will prevent the creation of any more School Boards. These committees may, if the County or Town Coun- cils appointing them think fit, contain a minority of co-opted members, that is, members who have never been elected by the ratepayers,yet it is proposed that they shall have the power to prevent any increase in the School Board rate. The great School Board of London will, for in- stance,have to receive the sanction of a committee of the London County Council before it can in- crease its rata by even a farthing in the pound. Yet the London School Board is elected by a direct popular vote while the committee for whose sanction they have to apply may possibly contain a large minority of co-opted members. T arrangement appears still more absurd when it is considered that the London School Board has a far larger revenue and controls a more numerous isttatffhrtohuangh a the CcoornunntlyittCeeo.unTciiil,ewho,ircehatis virtuetOcontroofl this elaborate contrivance in the eyes of this reac- tionary Government is doubtless the very fact that it will secure at least a partial abolition of the system of direct public control of education. Another advantage it possesses from the Tory point of view is, that it removes the

necessity for creating any more School Boards. The voluntary schools will now be fed with public funds through the Education Commit- tees, and in case of need the County or Borough Council will simply take over the administration and perform all the duties of management with the aid of voluntary managers. There are, it may be said, other parts of the 'Bill which will probably be less open to the criticism of enlightened educationists than the portion referring to the subsidising :of the elementary schools. It contains a scheme of secondary education which should be productive of benefit, though little can be done in this direc- tion unless funds are provided for the purpose and it is suggestive that the Bill omits to do this. The proposal that pauper and industrial children should be taken over by the new educational au- thorities and that the school age should be raised to twelve may also be steps in the right direction, but clearly these small matter will be a very poor compensation for the injuries that will be inflicted by a complete reversal of an educational system which has on the whole produced excellent results.

IN his speech at Huddersfield Lord Roseirry on Lord ROSEBERY made a search- Foreign Affairs. mg criticism of the extraordin- ary foreign policy of the present Government. No man can speak with more knowledge or more authority upon this subject than the late PREMIER, and the country will not feel reassured by what he has to say of Lord SALISBURY'S methods. Lord ROSEBERY rightly contends that this is a time for the concentration of the military resources of the Empire. It is many years since there was so much that is threatening and omin- ous in the foreign outlook as at the present time. One indication of this is the altogether unusual attention that is being paid at the pre- sent time to foreign affairs, and another is the almost feverish anxiety that is displayed by all the nations of Europe to strengthen their arma- ments and to develope all their military resources. And, as Lord ROSEBERY again pointed out, there are numerous questions awaiting settlement which might at any moment be the cause of inter- national quarrels. Yet this is the very time which our Government fixes upon for squandering its resources in an attempt to re-conquer the destrts of the Soudan. Lord ROSEBERY is evidently as much in the dark as everyone else outside the Cabinet as to the causes of the Nile expedition. He cautiously and charitably admits that there may be good reason for the expedition, ' but he is clear upon the point that no good reason has yet been given. The evil results that were bound to spring from this ill-timed adventure are now too apparent to be ignored. The of the French has been excited to such a pitch that the foreign ministers of that country have sk.arcely been able to keep their language within the limits of diplo- matic courtesy, and it probably really depends upon the extent to which France can carry Russia with her whether or not there is to be an open rupture between this country and the Republic on the other side of the Channel. It was surely hardly worth while bringing about this state of affairs for the sake of possessing a few thousand square miles of Soudanese sand. Even the members of the Government themselves seem now to be alarmed by the bigness of the bogey they have conjured up, and they are beginning to show that most fatal quality of weak administrators, indecision. It is to be hoped that the final outcome ot the Egyptian escapade may not be even more disas- trous to the reputation of the country than the Armenian fiasco.

THE MURDER OF A WARRINGTON WOMAN.

ACCUSED CHARGED WITH THEFT. At Manchester City Sessions on Monday, before the Recorder,Sir J. F. Lees,e, Q.C., M.P., Samuel Wilkinson, 48, gardener, was charged with stealing two bottlea of whisky and a cap from a golf club pavilion at Rusholme. He pl3aded guilty.—lt was stated that the prisoner had been committed,and is at present awaiting his trial, for murder,at Nottingham.—The Recorder said he was un- willing to make the position of the man more terrib' e than it already was. He under3tood that he was to take his trial for the crime of murder, committed at Notting- ham on the 18th July, and for the present offence he would be sentenced to imprisonment until that date.— Wilkinson made no reply, and during his short stay in the dock bore himself very firmly.

PLUCKY RESCUE.

On Sunday afternoon, two children named Betley re- siding in Wellington-st., Runcorn, were playing by the canal aide at the "Top Locks" when they both fell in the water. Their position was extremely perilous when a bystander in the person of Josoph Rinford plunged into the water and brought the children safely to the bank. There was a large crowd on the canal bank for some time, and the affair caused great excitement. The rescuer deserves every credit for his plucky dive. But for his assistance the children would certainly have been drowned. STREET ACCIDIINT AT RUNCORN.

A rather serious street accident took place an Friday afternoon, when a boy named Peter Daniels, of 43, Rut- land-street, was seriously injured by a cart which ran over him. The scholars were just leavine school and the boy named in crossing Bridge- itreet was knocked down by a cart. The lad was carried into the police station and first aid was promptly rendered by Superintendent Okell. Dr. McDougall was called in and after examina- tion had the lad conveyed home on the St. John's ambul- ance carriage. He was afterwards placed under the care of Dr. Robinson, and up to the time of writing he remained in a serious condition. TSB RUNCORN DISTRICT NURSING FUND. It will no doubt be remembered that when Miss Pier. point gave an entertainment a few months ago the re- ceipts were devoted to local charities and out of the pro- ceeds a sum of £lO was contributed to the Runcorn District Nurses Fund on the condition that it should be utilised for sending children out of town in cases where a change of air was considered necessary. It will be interesting to note that Nurse 13uckenham has made the first call upon the fund on behalf of James Ankers, aged ten years of 42, Loch-street, and tn boy is to be sent to the West Kirby Convalescent Home for a period of four weeks. The little fellow has suffered from hip disease for something like two years and has been for 13 months under the care of Dr. Murray of the Child- ren's Hospital, Liverpool. PREiENTATION TO A RUNCORN MANAGER. On Monday evening the staff and workmen of the Australian Main Company pro ented a handsome time- piece, Malacca cane, and illuminated address to Mr. William McCulloch, who has resigned his position as manager of the works. Mr.McCulloch came to the works five years ago to superintend the erection of machinery, and on the completion of that work remained as fore- ' man, and for the past twelve months as manager. His kindly disposition has won him the esteem of all with whom he came in contact, and he will leave behind him many friends. The presentation was made by Mr. D. Edwards (foreman), and Mr. J. Gaze (workman), who made a few appropriate remarks. The timepiece and cane bore a suitable inscription, and the address read as follows :—" Presented to William McCulloch, by the workmen of the Australian Alum Co., Runcorn, as a token of their respect and e,teem which he has won by the kindness and consideration he has always extended towards them during the period he has been connected with the works (latterly as manager) ; and they trust that he will accept the accompanying timepiece with their heartfelt wishes for his future welfare. (Signed) James Gazes, hon. secretary.—Mr. McCulloch said he could not express his appreciation of their gifts in words. He felt he was leaving friends behind him, who would occupy:a warm place in his heart. SUCCESS OF A RUNCORN ENGINEER IN QUEENSLAND. From time to time referenees have been made in the columns of the " Examiner " to the successful career of Mr. W. Wilson Litler in Rockhampton, Queensland. About five years ago Mr. Litter received an invitation from the ratepayers of Rockhampton to allow himself to be nominated on the City Council and at the election he defeated an older resident of the town who was also an ex-mayor. Since then Mr. Litter has filled the positio of alderman of the Council for three years and to that position he was re-elected on February Bth of this year. The week following the Council conferred upon him the highest honour at their disposal by electing him mayor. In Runcorn Mr.Litler's family connection will be known by most of the residents in the town. He is the son—the enly one—of Mr. Joseoh Litter, so well known as manager of the Runcorn dockyards of the Manchester Ship Canal. It was with his father that Mr. Litter served his apprenticehip its the service of the Bridgewater Canal Co. Shortly after the comple- tion ot his apprenticeship Mr. Litler migrated to Queens- land 13 years ago. In business there, he has met with considerable success. He established a foundry and en- gineering firm, known as Messrs. Litler and Bruce, very soon after arrival and it speedily attained success, and is well known in Queensland. It only remains to be said that Rockhampton is the most important city in central Queensland, and the hoeour of filling the Mayoral chair is no mean one,and the attaining of that honour stamps Mr. Litler as a man whom Runcorn may well be proud of.

The fortnightly meeting of this Council was held at the Workhouse, Dutton, on Tuesday afternoon. Present: Messrs. J. Mclntyre (chairman), H. E. Heywood, S. Woodward, C. Roylance, E. Aaron, T. MANCHESTER PORT SANITARZ AUTHORITY. A letter dated March 30th from Mr. S. B. Provis, assistant sezretary to the Local Government Board, was read as follows :—I am directed by the Local Govern- ment Board to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 25th inst., with reference to the proposed order for thP permanent constitution of a Port Sanitary Authority for the Port of Manchester and to state that the Board I have fully considered the representations made by the 1 Rural District Council of Runcorn at the enquiry recently i held at Manchester by their Inspector, Dr. Barry, but have not felt justified in deferring the issue of the order, having regard to the time which must elapse before the order becomes provisional in the event of objection being made to it, and to the period within which the Bill for the comfirmation of a provisional order must be intro- duced into Parliament if the order is to be confirmed during the present session. Mr. Heywood considered that the matter was of great importance to them as a council. The estimated ex- penditure of the authority would be about £l,BOO a year and they would have one twentieth to find.—lt was a serious matter for them as a Rural District Council and in his opinion they would receive no benefit whatever. It would be still more serious for the Runcorn Urban District Council, for on their rateable value it would mean lfd. in the £, the authority was thrust upon them by the corporations of Manchester and Salford, and he thought they ought to protest against it.—Mr. Williams was in favour of the memorial of protest previously agreed upon going forward. Mr. Aaron seconded, and the resolution was: carried. It was decided to affix the seal of the board as SOOII as possible.

WEbTON SEWERS.

Mr. F. A. Lake, law clerk to the Runcorn Urban Dis- trict Council, wrote as follows :—" The correspondence between Mr. Diggle, yourself, and Mr. Wilding, our Sur- veyor en the above matter was considered by my com- mittee this morning. lam instructed to call your atten- tion to the fact that there has already been a breach of the agreement of the Bth January,lBBs,by the extension of your sewer beyond the limits therein referred to. My clients have no desire to do anything unneighbourly. and therefore by coming to any decision as to terminat- ing the said agreement or otherwise would be obliged if you will supply them with a plan showing the extent ef the proposed extension of the sewer beyond the point shown on the plan annexed to the agreement—the el- tent of the district it is proposed to drain by means of it both in the present and future, and the number of houses therein. On receipt of this information my clients will be in a position to consider any proposals your council may have to make with a view to some new arrangement, which my clients are of opinion will be necessary, and should be settled within the next three months. It was decided to provide the plans desired. THE ALLEGED NUISANCE AT FRODSRAM STATION. With reference to the above alleged nuisance at Frodsham Station, Mr. C. E. Linaker wrote saying that the provision the Railway Company bad hitherto adopted had not resulted in abating the nuisance in the slightest degree. He had frequently known the carting of bones to go on all day long. He hoped the Council would not let this matter rest, but would compel the company to make other provisions for the trafficin order to give Mr. Mason (of the Railway Company) more information than he appeared to get from his officials, he would send to the Clerk of the Council each week the number of trucks which arrived and the time they welt, cleared. In conclusion, Mr. Linaker inquired if he would strengthen the hands of the Counzil if be got two medical men of Frodsham to certify that the nuisance as it existed was injurious to health. Mr.Hadfleld,of Liverpool,writing on the same subject, sharacterised the proceedings of the Railway Company of giving notice and taking the trucks to Helsby, as absurd, as they were non•effective. He was now in consultation with his lawyers as to compelling either.the Council or the Railway Company to seek a proper re- medy by way of a siding to abate the nuisance. It vas moved and seconded and carried that the clerk send a copy of the letters to Mr. Mason. The Clerk was also authorised to communicate with Mr. Linaker to see the medical gentlemen of the distrizt and get them to give Dr. Adams their opinion as to whether the alleged nuisance was injurious to health. THE FREE TRADE QUESTION.

A letter was read from the Whitchurch Union enclos- ing a ccpy of a petition which thoy had caused to be presented to the House of Commons pointing out the in- jurious effect of uarestricted foreign competition on trade and agriculture,and urgiag that the present fiscal system of the country was largely responsible for the continued depression and distress. They asked the Board to pass a similar oetition.—Mr. Hey- worla proposed that the petition be signed. —Mr. Aaron seconded the resolution.—Dr. Gastaldi supported, and hoped that those goods made in Germany' would soon be taxed.—Mr.Woodward proposed as an amendment that the letter lie on the table. The question of free trade wa., too wide for the Board to go into that day.—Mr. %Villains seconded.—On the matter being put to a vote only two voted for the amendment and the original resolution was declared carried. THE CLERK'S REMUNERATION FOR ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE WORK.

Mr. Heywood reported that the Assessment Commit- tee had held a meeting that morning aril recommended that the guardians pay the clerk £lOO per annual for his services as clerk to the Assessment Committee. The salary previously had been £B5 and in recommend- ing the increase the committee had taken into consider- ation that three new towcships had been added to the Union, and also that during the whole time Mr. Ashton had been clerk there had been no law expenses. In the past when there had been extra work the committee had granted extra renumeration and last year he was paid £5O. On the present occasion the committee recom- mended that the clerk be paid £2O for extra work.— Mr. Aaron seconded the adoption of the recommendation. Mr. Ashton had done a great deal of work and the amount of £l2O was less than it had been for 20 years. —Mr. Woodward pointed out that in the previous year the committee recommended a sum of £135. so that the amount recommended this year was £l5 less.—The Chairman informed the Council that the average amount paid had been £145 a year.—The recommendation was unanimously accepted.

RUNCORN 'URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

ELECTION.

RESULT OF THE POLL.

On Saturday the poll was taken in the three wards in which there were contests for the vacant seats on the Runcorn Urban District Council. Throughout the con- test little or no interest has been evinced in the elec- tions and the fact was conclusively proved by the poll- ing on Saturday when only about half of the electors ex- ercised their right of recording their votes. The candi- dates in Bridgewater Ward were Dr. McKenzie who sought re-election, ana Mr. W. Handley who had occu- pied a seat on the old Board of Commissioners. The voting was from twelve o'clock at noon to 8 p.m. and the Bridgewater booths were situated at the National Schools, Church-street, number 1, being, in charge of Mr. James Wilding, with Mr. Austin as poll clerk, and number 2 was in charge of Mr. Marshall with Mr. Nall as clerk. The number of electors for this ward, is 645 out of which 301 went to the poll. In Mersey Ward the fight lay between Mr. J.llorefleld and Mr. Charles Dutton, both of these gentlemen having never enjoyed public honours. The polling booths were situated at Holy Trinity Schools. No. 1, being in charge of Mr. W. Jones, with Mr. W. Howard as clerk, and No. 2, in the charge of Me. J. Nickson, with Mr. Pearson as cleric. The electorate numbers 596, out of which 312, recorded their votes three of which, at the count were found to be spoiled. In the Halton Ward, Mr. F. Wiswall the retiring mem- ber, was opposed by Mr. E. Gatley, secretary of the Waterman's Association. The ward numbers 420 elec- tors, but only 259 took the trouble to give their votes. The polling booth was at Mill Brow, and Mr. J. D. Jones presided with Mr. G. S. Orme as clerk. The polling in all the wards was very slow and the officials were not heavily pressed. Counting was com- menced at Halton Ward by Mr. Lake and shortly after 8 o'clock he was able to announce the result as follows

HALTON WARD.

Mr. Wiswall Mr. Gatley

Majority At the next ward Mr. Lake found everything ready and the work was carried out expeditiously. The fol- lowing is the re3ult of the

MERSEY WARD.

B R IDGRWATER WARD.

Majority At each polling station, votes of thanks were accorded to the returning officers and the officials.

WESTON PARISH COUNCIL.

RESUME OF THE YEAR'S WORK.

Weston Parish meeting was held on Friday evening in the Church schoolroom, and was largely attended. On the motion of Mr. Guest, seconded by Mr. Vose, Mr. Sill, was appointed chairman. In opening the proceedings, the Chairman said the business done by the Parish Council last year was not much, but they had been feeling their way in a quiet :nanner. They had not rushed to do great things, but they had done several little pieces of work for the good of the pariel. In the first place,there arose the question of the water supply. The Runcorn Waterworks Com- pany were bound by Act of Parliament to give Weston a supply of water. The undertaking was recently taken over by the Runcorn Urban authority,l and last year the residents of Weston had grave cause for complaints. The Parish Council wrote to the Runcorn Council and the consequence had been that since then the parish had had a very 'good supply, and during the latter mentbs of the year they had had no cause for complaint. Another matter taken up was that of the state of the roads at Weston Point. It was under discussion by the Highway Board for a number c f years but there was evidently some block in the way. The sub- ject was brouaht before the Parish Council, and owing to the representations they made to the District Coun- cil, arranaements were now being mad,: for an improved road at Weston Point, and he believed also for better sewerage arrangements. They had also been going in for deposit for niat t soil. The Council held several meetings, and met a deputation of the District Council on that subject. An arrangement was come to, and when it had received the consent of the Local Govern- ment Board, the work would be carried out and a sanit- ary want in the parish would have been provided. No one in the parish had made an application for allotments. If some one had done so it weuld have been the duty of the Council to consider the application.

THE ORRED CHARITY.

The statement of accounts of this charity showed that the amount received was £2B 2. Of this £27 Os. 6d. was distriblted in doles, and after meeting other expenses there was a balance in hand of 163. 61 —Mr. Ball said according to the Act the names of the recipients should be issued.—The Chairman said the Council agreed last year to publish the names in the post offices at Weston Point and Weston. He did not hink it would be fair to read the names of the rec:pients at that meeting. He pointed out that the Parish Council would have the privilege of appointing one trustee for the charity, and the Vicar another. The Council had approached the Charity Commissioners on the subject but up to the present they had net received a reply.—ln reply to a question by Mr. Somerville, the Chairman ' said he had not got a list of the beneficiaries of the charity. Mr. Somerville then moved that the trustee of I the charity be asked to furnish a list of the recipients to the parish meeting. This was seconded and carried. 4.,

AN ILLEGALITY AT TEIE LAST MEETING.

The Chairman read a letter from Mr. Somerville stat- ing that he would move at the next meeting the follow- ing resolution :—" That the Clerk of the Parish Council on behalf of this meeting do forthwith apply to the County Council of Cheshire for an order calling a parish meeting in lieu of the parish meeting for Weston Ward held on the 9th inst., on the ground that such meeting held on the 9th inst. did not comply with the Local Government Act, 1894, inasmuch as the Chairman at such meeting was and is not a parochial elec- tor, and therefore ineligible to act as chairman,and that this resolution be communicated by the Clerk of the Parish Cpuncil to the returning officer."—The Chairman said after carefully studying the communication he ' ruled it out of order. It was a question which tho parish meeting had nothing to do with. The responsibility of deciding whether the last parish eeting was legal or illegal was in the hands of the returning officer. The onus was thrown upon him,and if after the electicn any person felt himself aggrieved he could on his own res- ponsibility and expense petition against the return of the members of the Council. The returning officer had written as follows on the question : "Dear Sir,—Parish Council Election. In reply to your inquiry I consider that Article 13 of the second Schedule of the Parish Council Election Order 1896 pro- vides for the case you have submitted to nse, and that the election cannot be declared invalid by reason of non• compliance with Article 3 of the said order."—Contiou- ing the Chairman said those who were present at the last meeting referred to. knew it was conducted in com- pliance with the act.—Mr.Somerville contended that the last meeting did not cnmplv with the act,and was,there- fore illegal.—The Clerk (Mr. Collier), admitted that he was a little in error in not taking the chair himself.— The subject then dropped, and the meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the Chairman.

CAMEL-HAIR BEL TING. —REDDAWAY v. BANHAM IN THRIIIJCISE OF LORDS, MARCH 26TFI, 1898. BY JUDGMENT delivered this day their Lordships unanimously reversed the deciaion of the Court of Appeal and comfirmed the findings of the jury on the trial of the Action. The questions left to the Jury and their answers thereto were as follows : Q. " Does ' Camel-hair Belting' mean beltinr. made tiv the Plaintiffs as distinguished from belting - made by any other manufacturers ?"—A. "YES." Q. "Or doeslit mean belting of a particular kind without reference to any particular maker ?"—A.. "NO."

Q. "Do the Defendants so describe their belting. as to be likely to mislead purchasers and to lead them to buy the Defendants' belting as aud for the belting of the Plaintiffs ?"—A. "YES." Q. "Did the Defendants endeavour to pass off their gaok-ls as and for the goods of the Plaintiff, so as to be likely as to deceive purchasers ?".—A. " YES." Their Lordships ordered a Perpetual Injunction, and that the Defendants should pay the costs of the Action' and Appeals.

MAKE LIFE ENJOYABLE.

Loss of health means loss of all which makes life en- joyable ; for, in addition to the bodily pain and suffering which it cause?, it entails a vast amount of mental pain and anxiety, especially in the homes of the working classes. It brings in its train a host of discomforts which retard the recovery of the sufferer, and hinder, instead of assisting, Nature to perform her functions. When a working man suffers from any ailment which prevents his following his daily avocation, he is natur- ally filled with anxiety regarding the maintenance of those depending on him for their daily sustenance. His very love for his family retards and hinders his speedy recovery, and his spirits become depressed at the antici- pation of the gloomy prospect; before them in the event of a long continuance of his illness. It is well known that depression of spirits often proves a serious bar to a speedy recovery. <;- These results arise in addition to the various incon- veniences and discomforts which the sufferer and his family experience as direct results of his illness, such as additional expenses for luxuries for the sick, with in- creased difficulty to obtain those luxuries, or even the necessities of life. Mast of the various forms of disease which trouble the labouring classes of the country, and which are often attributable, more or less, to some unhealthly condition of their daily work, may be classed under a few leading heads.

To avoid thq discomforts arising irom various forms of disease,every working man should seek the aid of

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

APPLICATION TO ALTER AN ORDER FOR WIFE DESERTION.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Friday morning, Mr. Arthur Browne appeared on behalf of Edward Poynton to ask for a variation of an order made a fort- night previously upon him to contribute 6s. weekly to- wards the maintenance of his wife Ann Poynton tor deserting her. The grounds of the application were that the applicant's means were insufficient and that the wife had ample means of her own. Mr. Browne wished to bring fresh evidence, as the husband was not repre- sented at the first hearing, tut it was ruled that this could not be done. He then proceeded to state that as Poynton had never previously been in court, he did not know how to act, and had not cross-examined his wife as to her means. He would call evidence to prove that Mrs. Poynton was in receipt of an average profit of Bs. per week from a shop, and that she received 12s. from one son,s3.from another and 63. per week from a lodger, making in all 31s. per week, and her husband had also left part of the furniture. Poynton only got 203. per week wages and therefore could not afford to pay the order. Evidence in support of this statement was given by the applicant and a son Henry Poynton.—Mr.Davies denied that Mrs.Poynton got Bs. per week from the shop as it had only been n small sweet shop which was now given up altogether. She had to keep the three who gave her money out of what they gave her, and the son who gave her 12s. per week would shortly be married and she would then lose that.—The magistrates without hearing any evidence from Mrs. Poynton, confirmed the rder.

A LOCAL CASE OF "RUNNING AMUCK."

At the Warrington Co .rt, on Monday, before the Mayor and Alderman Harrison, Julia Ann Shields, of Dial Court,was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Bridge-st., on Saturday night, and further charged with assaulting Margaret Carter and Mary Jane Cald- well on the same occasion.—Mrs. Carter said she was walking with Mrs. Caldwell on Saturday night, when the prisoner,without the least provocation, first struck Mrs. Caldwell and then herself (witness). She struck witness while she had a baby in her arms, and dragged her to the floor by the hair. Prisoner was very drunk at the time.—P.C. Monks (No. 38), stated that when he apprehended the prisoner she made use of disgusting languagc.—The Chief Constable : Her conduct, your worships,while she has been in the cells has been scanda- lous. She has teen more like a wild beast than a woman. —John Brandwood said that prior to the assault the women he also was struck by the prisoner. The two complainants were coming down the street at the time, and as they were passing the prigoner ran at Mrs. Cald- well, and knocked her to the floor. Then she ran at Mrs. Carter and struck her. —The Chief Constable : It is a sort of "running amuck," I should think, your wor- ships. She has been 30 times before the magistrates for different offences, and has bad one month's imprison- ment frequently. Nothing seems to do her much good. —Prisoner : I must be very dangerous.—The Chief Constable : You are really.—The Mayor : It seems as though she would rather live in prison than out of it.— The Mayor: Well, for being drunk and disorderly you will have one month's imprisonment ; and for the as- saults two months—three months altogether. McoNLIGHTING" AT WARRINGTON.-VIOLENT IRISHMEN.

Before the Mayor and Alderman Harrison at the War- rington Borough Court on Monday,Thomas and Edward O'Brien were charged with being drunk aucl disorderly In Buttermarket-street at 10.10 on Sunday night; they were also charged with assaulting Patrick Walsh in Butterrnarket-street at the same time; John Hines was charged with obstructing the police while effecting the arrest of the O'Briens at the same time and place.—P.C. Corcoran said that on the night in question he received a complaint from Mrs. Walsh about her husband being assaulted. He proceeded towards Bank-st. and found a disorderly crowd in Buttermarket-st. The two O'Briens were drunk and quarreling. Thomas was taken to the Police S ation and locked up by witness and Edward by P.C. Bell. Hines was there but not drunk.—P.C. Chrimes said he saw Edward O'Brien drunk in the street and with P.C. Bell locked him up. Hines followed them and Jostled them trying to rescue O'Brien. .When they ar.. rived at the police station they ran him in als,.—P.C. Bell corroborated. He said there a as a crowd of from 300 to 400 persons about the street. It was disgraceful for a Sunday night. They heard the O'Briens shouting as far as the Central Station.—Patrick Walsh,who walked lame and bore other evidence of rough h tndli ng,said on Sunday night the men came at him like roaring lions. One held him while the other kicked him. He knew them pre- viously hut he haJ had no quarrel with them They owed him money. He said the men were dangerous to be at largo in the streets.—The Chief Constable re- marked that this was a little bit of moonlighting in Eng- land.—Mrs. Walsh said she heard the noise and came out and saw her husband being assaulted. Edward O'Brien was holding him and Thomas kicking and strik- ing. She went to them and told them to desist. Thomas O'Brien then struck her. She went for the police.—The magistrates said the conduct of the defendants was most disgraceful, and they would send the O'Briens to gaol for a month each for the drunkenness and another month for the assault. —Hines was fined 20s. and costs or in default 14 days' hard labour._

A. FISH DEALER'S FREAK.

At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Joseph Lever, a fishmonger, or Warrington, was charged with having at Stretton, assaulted and beaten Catherine Robinson, on the 19th March, and alsc with having on the same date unlawfully and wilfully spoilt a quantity of fish the property of Catherine Robinson, of the value of 18s. The defendant pleaded not guilty.—Mr. F. R. Roberts, solicitor, Warrington, who prosecuted, in stat- ing the case, said the complainant was a fish hawker. living in Warrington, and for a number of years had been in the habit of coming in that district selling fish, and was regarded as a highly respectable woman. The defendant was a wholesale and retail fish dealer having a stall in Warrin:ton market. He was looked upon in tbe light of a hawk invading a dovecote. He bad been underselling the old established fish dealers in the mar- ket, and had caused a great deal of ill-feeling. But the complainant had had nothing to do with this rivalry. On the date named, a horse and trap was standing at the door of Mr. Grea we's public-house at Stretton, and on complainant passing the horse's head in order to get to the hotel where she hoped as usual to sell fish, the de- fendant met her. He was undoubtedly the worse for drink. He struck her a nasty blow on the head, and knocked ker basket of fish which she carried on her head to the ground. The fish was scattered in all direc- tions. Mrs. Robinson was stunned by the blow, but when she came to her senses, she found her fish in the mud and some friend gathered it together. When it had been replaced in the basket, tbe defendant not satisfied with the damage he had done, taok a run- ning kick at the basket sending it and the fish flying once more into the mud, this time rendering the fish worthless. She asked him what he had done it for, and told him to be honourable and pay for it. He refused to do so, and used some disgusting language. The woman remained in the vicinity of the public-house for some time, and on the defendant leaving, he offered her a shilling. She took the money, but when she got home she had to throw the fish away. There were two wit- nesses who saw the assault, but the woman was too poor to snbpcene them. Oce of them, an organgrinder,called at his (Mr. Roberts) office, and told him that he teen

NORTHERN UNION. WARRINGTON v. BRIGHOUSE RANGERS.

The return match between the above teams was played on the Rangers' ground on Saturday, The long journey was commenced at 10 50 a.m.,and on approach- ing Manchester the rain came down in torrents, and the apprehensions of the players as to the state of the ground for play were increased when they got near to the Pennine chain, for the rain turned to snow, which came down in large flakes. On getting into Yorkshire, however, the weather improved, and by the time Brig- house was reached it was very fine but 'Cold, 'and this kind of weather continued during the rest of the afternoon, with the exception of one short shower. The Brighouse ground has a steep slope from one Corner diagonally to the opposite corner, and the team playing down the slope has an enormous advantage. This advan- tage was further increased on Saturday, by a gale of wind blowing down the slope, and also a blinding sun in the eyes of the team playing up the field. Winning the toss was an important consideration under these circumstances, and this fell to Brighouse, who thus had the advantage of hpth slope, wind and the sun the first half. The Rangers fie a big lot, and Used their weight with such effcrt that the Warringtonians, when ends were changed, were,well,not so fresh as they might have been. There was a good gate for Brighouse consider- ing the size of the town, there being about 1,500 spectators, and the following were the two teams : Warrington ; Heesom, full back; Carey, cross, O'Brien, and Lawless, threequarters • Bate (captain), and Hig- ginbottom, half backs ; Dakin, Berry, Sudlow, Wilcock, Fairhurst, Scholtze, Saunders, and Andrews. Brighouse Rangers: Eaglss, full back ; Waddington, Hartley, Brooke, and Abbey, threequarters ; Schofield and Ross, half backs ; Croft. Wood, A. G. Sugden, Jagger, Armi- tage, Earnshaw, Pullman, and Hughes,forwards. Dakin kicked off for Warringtan,!and after a return by Ross, the Rangers forwards rushed down the field in a body, and during the first three Minutes. two minors were forced. A mueh more favourable complexion was put upon the game, when Dakin and Fairhurst dribbled up the field,and before Lewis Brooke could get his kick in, he was well tackled by O'Brien. Warrington continued to work their way up the field but when least expected, disaster came to them. Ross made his mark near his own 25 flag ,and following up his kick,he made his mark again in response to Heesom's return kick. This was near the touch line midway between the centre and quarter lines,but Wood was successful in kicking a fine four points goal from this position. The Rangers now put pressure on to some tune, 'and Wood was grounded b. Dakin just on the line. Another free kick from a knock on was taken by Wood,but this time the ball went wide,and only a minor resulted. Cross and O'Brien put their side in a more favourable position, and from a scrimmage at"centre, Bate made A brilliant run almost to the line before Waddingtoa grassed him. This res- pite was only momentary, for after the ensuing scrim- mages from a pass from Bate, O'Brien dropped a neat goal although he was tackled at the same time. Immedi- ately after the kick off, a fine kick was taken by Wood, and although it went wide, Hartley followed up so well that be was allowed a try on the ground that he bad been obstructed by Heesom. Wood failed to kick a goal. From now to near half time, Warrington were completely overplayed, but they defended gamely. Heesom was especially prominent by his 'fearless tackl- ing, and twice in one minute, he bowled over the burly, Wood at the cornersflig. A good round of passing be- tween Bate, Cross and Carey, almost ended in the latter getting clear, but he was tackled from behind by Brooke and soon after, the whistle blew for half time with rhe score, Brighouse, one dropped goal, one try, Warrington, one try. Wood restarted the game and Lawless's return did not neutralise the kick off. The second half opened very quietly, neither team appearing to put much dash in their play. Briehouse, however, gradu- ally gained ground, and at length, Pullman got over and scored a very soft try. Exchange kicking between Lawless and Eagles ended to the advantage of the former, and for a time pay settled in home quarters but Ross, Abbey, Brookes, and Hartley were the means of tilting the ball back, and for the fourth successive Saturday,Waddington scored, but Pullman failed at eoal. After the restart, Carey had a drop at goal which fell short, but Warrington by means of good foot work by Dakin, Fairhurst, Saunders and Sudlow, kept up the attack, and Eagles mulling the ball,Wilcock scored with- out much difficulty. Higginbottem failed to make the try of any increased value. The rest of the game was all in favour of Warrington. Eagles again mulled the ball, and Dakin and Saunders were just in the act of taking the ball, when Eagles in a nsost inexplicable manner found the ball in his hands and kicked luckily in touch. Probably no one wag more surprieed than Eagles at this result, and it was a very lucky let off for Brighcuss. Lawless also on one occasion had a good run down touch, but Waddington chucked his career, and no score came. When the whistle blew, Brighouse were returned victors by a dropped goal and three tries (1.5 points) to Warrington a dropped goat and one try (seven points).

RUNCORN v. WIGAN.

These well-known_rivals met at Runcorn on Saturday. Considerable interest was evinced in the match and about 4,000 spectators were present. In the Wigan ranks Blackburn and Forster officiated in the place of Brown and Hallintel' while for the Runcorn team West was substituted for Warder, who is on the injured list. Playing with the sun in front Wigan were quickly on the defensive, but Be keen were the three-quarters that the passing movements of the home team were quickly stopped and the game was mostly kept in the hands of the forwards. Foster started a passing movement at centre and after Jago and Wilding had handled the ball Flowers sprinted along touch and was only stopped by Butterworth in the nick of time. Quickly following this, a general scramble on the line gave Flowers an opening which he took advantage of. Winstanley failed to con- vert. The Wigan halves and three-quarters were penal- ized at times for off-side play, and Farmer made several attempts at goal from good places. A dreary round of " scrums " was varied by a good passing run by Nickson, J. Faulkner and Butterworth. The latter got over but a scrummage was ordered. A bout of passing followed between Robinson and J. Faulkner and West ended by punting over Winstanley's head but that player managed to touch down. Although Runcorn had had nearly the whole of the play they ended the first half three points in the arrear. The second portion of the game was composed almost entirely of uninteresting scrummages. The Run- corn halves had more frequently possession, but the visiting threequarters were standing practically on a line with"the scrummage, and the home combination was completely broken up. The penalties inflicted upon Wigan for off-side play, brought no appreciable advant- age to the homesters. For fifteen minutes the ball did not veer ten yards either side of the centre flag, until the visiting team went off in a loose dribble. The Run- corn men made a poor attempt to stop them, but Dixon managed to get on at the corner. For the remaining minutes of the game the visitors had the upper hand and retired winners by two tries to nothing. The game speak- ing generally was one of the poorest exhibitions seen this season on the Runcorn field. The Wigan backs laid them- selves out to stop the passing movements of the home team, and did so in defiance of all rules relating to off side.Tne game was made up of scrummages and penalty kicks, and the visitors were penalized at least twenty times. Had Runcorn bad a good kicker at least three goals would have been landed. As it was Runcorn missed many chances in that direction, and the game counts as a loss to the home team. The teams were :—Runcorn: Full back, Hough; threeqUarters, West Myer, Butter- worth, and Robinson ; half backs, Faulkner and Nick- son ; forwards, Farmer, Langley. Hughes, Darlington, Walker, Moores, Williams, and W. Faulkner. Wigan : Full back, Winstanley ; threequarters, Flowers, Jago, Wilding, and Dixon'; half backs, Foster and Walker; forwards. Unsworth, Rigby, Webster, Benson, Yates, Blackburn, Green, and Perkins.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

FRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on Friday last week Mr. CURZON, in reply to a question put by Mr. W. Allen, said the Government had no intention of sending another expedition to Dongola in the autumn. Answering Mr. Labouchere, Mr. Curzon said it WAS understood that the French Government did not consider that a vote of the majority of the Commissioners of the Public Debt in Egypt was sufficient for the grant towards the cost of the expedition to Dongola. Sir W. HARCOURT asked what the situation was with respect to the payment. Mr. Ctrazox replied that the Commissioners had decided by four to two to grant the sum asked for. The French and Russian Commissioners had protested against that decision, and certain French bondholders had served a notice on the Commissioners protesting against any pay- ment from the reserve fund for the expenses of the expedition, and summoning them before the tribunals on the 13th of April. Mr. A. J. BALFOUR informed the House that the Duke of Cambridge bad declined to allow the proposal for a pension is respect of his services as Commander-in-Chief P 0 be submitted to the House.

In the House of Lords Lord WEMYSS moved the'second reading of a bill to secure uniformity in the granting,by way of renewal or transfer, of liquor licenses. He rejected all suggestions to withdraw the bill for the present. The Duke of DEVONSHIRE moved the adjourn- ment of the debate, and the motion was agreed to by 56 votos against seven. A bill brought in by Lord Burton dealing with offences against the licensing laws was also practically thrown out.

MONDAY.

• In the House of Commons on Monday Mr. H. lisciarrT called attention to a pamphlet which stated, on the authority of Board of Trade statistics, that the nullbers of persons killed and injured in industrial accidents dur— ing the year 1895 were 4,533 and 27:197.respective1y,and asked the Home Secretary whether he would at once in- troduce the promised Employers' Liability Bill, in order to diminish the risks. Sir M. White Ridley said he was unable to indicate the precise date when he could intro- duce the Employers' Liability Bill. He might, however, state that he should very shortly introduce a Coal Mines Regulation 13111, which waa directly intended to save life. Mr. Curzon informed Mr. W. Reimond that the reported purchase of Delagoa Bay was "entirely with-- out foundation

In reply to Mr. Bryce, Mr. CuazoN said the Govern- ment understood that, the Sultan having requested the Khedive to express his views with regard to the advance of Egyptian troops in the valley of the Nile, the Khedive bad stated in reply that the Egyptian and British Governments had agreed that the moment was op- portune for endeavouring to bring back Dongola under Egyptian administration, and that an ex- pedition had accordingly been despatched thither. On the motion for the third reading of the Naval Works Bill, Mr. W.REDMOND moved the rejection of the bill on the ground that the people of Ireland would get no benefit from the expenditure. Alter some debate the amendment was rejected by 186'votes to 27,and the bill was then read a third time.
