TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday Mr. CURZON, in reply to Mr. Leuty, said the reports from consular offi- cers in Asia Minor showed that in some districts there was no reason to apprehend a renewal of disorder. Mr. BALFOUR, in answer to Mr. W. Johnston, gave the terms of the reference to the Royal Commission on the Liquor Traffic and the names of twenty-three out of the twenty-four Commissioners. Viscount Peel, the late Speaker, is to be the Chairman of the Commission. Sir J. GoRsT, in asking leave to introduce a bill "to make further provision for education in England and Wales." reminded the !louse at the outset of some of the difficulties in education which the wisdom of Parlia- , merit would, somehow or other, have to surmount. There was first the difficulty of Voluntary schools. Last year, out of every seven children educated, three were educated in Board schools and four in Voluntary schools. In 1870 the Church of England was responsible for the education of 844,000 children ;, last year the number was 1,850.000. Church subscriptions in 1870 amounted , to £329,000 ; last year they were £640,000 ; Roman Catholics and very many members of the Church of Eng- land made it a point of conscience that their children should be educated by teachers of their own donomina-' tion, and it would be impo=sible to force those children out of their own schools without being guilty of a piece of religious intelerance to which the people of England in these enlightened days would never consent. Another solid obstacle to the disappear- ance of the Voluntary schools was the cost of replacing them. He was advised that it would entail a capital expenditure of over £25.000,000 and an annual charge for maintenance of £2,282,000. Therefore, so far as practical statesman- ship was concerned, the question to consider was not whether Voluntary schools should be abolished, but whether they were efficient, and, if not, how they could best be made so. After referring to the state of rural, secondary, and technical education. Sir Join; said the chief provisions of the'bill were a grant of 4s. for every child in all Voluntary and-some Board schools, the aboli- tion ot the 17. ed. limit, the raising of the age of com- pulsory full-time attendance to twelve years, the addi- tion to the "conscience clause" of a clause directing that one of the conditions on which every elementary school should receive public money should be that if a reasonable number of parents required to have separate religious instruction given to their children it was to be the duty of the managers of the school to permit all reasonable arrangements to be made for allowing that religious instruction to be given ; the creation of a para- mount educational authority in every county and borough, which should be the one channel through which public money was distributed, and which should have the administration also of the special grants for technical and secondary education; the exemption of Voluntary schools from rating ; and the giving of power to the rat- ing authority to check the increase in School Board expenditure. Mr. AeLAND described the bill as the most enormous change in our educational system this conntry had ever seen. After some debate leave was given to bring in the bill, which was then brought in and read a first time. A bill for the registration of teachers was ' also brought in and read a first time, and the House then adjourned until Thursday, April 9.

DEATH OF A WELL-KNOWN ATHLETE.

Sebastian H. Kendrick, aged 51 years, died at his residence, Lacy-street, Widnes, on Monday night. At one tithe deceased was one of the most promin- ent athletes in the county and his connection with the Widnes Football Club extended over a great many years. He figured in the first match played by that club of which he was a member up to eight years ago. As an athlete he was very suc- cessful and won prizes for races from the 100 yards sprint to a quarter of a mite and three years ago won the veteran's 100 yards handicap at Bebington. His interest in sport never flagged, and he attended the Runcorn and Wigan match last ,Saturday. He had been ailing for some time hut the immediate cause of death was a sharp attack of pleurisy.

CITRIOIIB DROWNING FArALITr AT RUNCORN.

THE INQUEST.

On Monday afternoon Mi. J. Worsley held an inquest at the Police Station, Runcorn, touching the death of John Newton, a labourer, residing in Windbill-st.,, who was found drowned in the canal on Saturdayinight. Thomas Newton, re:Ming in Windmill-st., Runcorn, said deceased was his son aged 23 years. He was a labourer,but had been out of work for about TA:months. Deceased lived with' him. On Saturday night bis.son was brought home about nine o'clock by a man. He was the worse for drink. His other son, Thomas. came soon after.and the two men had a bit of bother. Thomas was drunk ana John wanted to fight. He prevented his sons from fighting. John then went into the yard and called for his other son to come out. Thomas refused,and when he (witness) went into the yard he found deceasediha d gone. He afterwards heard that he was drowned. The canal was about 50 yards from his house. Deceased ieft his coat and vest in the yard. Thomas Newton.a brother of the deceased,said that he found his brother in his father's house an Saturday night. They were both drunk and he did not remember what the quarrel was about. Deceased went into the yard and asked him to come out and fight. Deceased had never talked about drowning himself • or taking' away his life. _ _

Frederick Bushall said he lodged with the father ef the deceased and was in the house when both .I3hn and Thomas Newton came in. Both were drunk and they got talking about football. One thing led t ) another and deceased went outside to fight Tom but Toth would not go out. He did not see anything more of the' de- ceased. Deceased would not be sober enough to know what he was doing. He had seen deceased sitting on the wall abutting the canal and he was in the habit of doing so.

Mary Harrison, a girl living in Queenland-st., said she was going by the canal and saw a man put his arms on the wall. He stayed in that position for two minutes and then got .on the wall. He lay on the wall for a short time and she afterwards heard a splash in the water. She raised an alarm. She heatd deceased muttering to him- self.

William Shaw, seamen's missionary, at Runcorn, said that he was called abouttwenty minutes to ten a nd re- covered the body. He consideled that it would be easy to rescue the man if anyone had been there. The Coroner: That's what I have been thinking. Could you not find anyone who was there at the time? P.f.l). Gregory. Na sir :. but I might say, that it is a very dangerous place. A juryman present has saved two lives there.

The foreman of the jury - in giving the verdict said that they were of an opinion that deceased went to sit upon the wall and fell into the water; is verdict of "'Accidental drowning" was therefore returned.

REPAYMENT OF RELIEF.—wARRINGTON VERSUS WIGAN.

Tne CHAIRMAN aked the Clerk to read a short staie- rnent made by Mr. Jonner Fast at the last meeting of the Wigan Board of Guardians. It was comparing a very important department of the Warrington Union with the samedepartra.nt at Wigan, very much to the advantage of Warrington He 'bought it was but their duty to hrweit read, and give credit to their officers when it was due.—ln the statement alluded to Mr. Jenner Fast after quoting statistics IV-lowing that the cost of relief in the Wigan union Vja qj.-'sing at an alarming rate, the auditor compared the Wigan system and results with the system and results °Drained in the Warrington Union. He remarked th,t in Warrington they had one collector, whilst in the Wigan Union the relieving officers did the collecting. The amount of relief r,enverei in the Wigan Union from relatives was £445, and of that only £BB .was re- covered in respect of out-door relief given. The amount recovered taken as a whole was 1.88 per cent. of the ex- penditure. In Warrington the expenditure was £11,805 and the amount T ecn vend £998 or a per centage of 836: He pointed out that if the e4lection in Wigai had been at the same rate as in Warrington £1,9701 would have been recovered instead of £445. The comp irisons between the two unirns was rather a striking one. It might be that they did not take the same view of the desirability of recovering from relatives that the War- rington Guardians did, but he would remind them that the result of not making relatives pay who could Day, was that they made others pay who were not relatives which seemed hardly fair. He thought it would be wise to consider whether it would not be advisable to appoint someone to collect throughout the tininn at a per cent- age, and also to attend to the removal of lunatics.—The Chairmon : That is a very great compliment. I think the Board may share it. (Laughter.)

It was, however, all the more i necessary for ratepayers when increases in the demand!' n aid of the poor were made upon them, to avoid misapprehensions by careful examination of particulars of the rate which had long been treated as en omnibus rate, and been made to carry burdens altogether foreign to the name of poor rate.—The estimates were adopted. BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN

The information contained in this book ought, to be known by every married woman, and it will not harm the unmarried to read. The book is c ,nveniently divided into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty, or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation that one can ordinarily get only from an in- telligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a. rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixtb chapter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them. The seven; h chapter treats cf maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due tolongings on the part of the mother, bat rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh ()fleeter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result. The last chapter treats of the "change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much Is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining hapniness. The book can be had from Dr. T• R. ALLINSON, 25 Box, 4, bpanish-place Manchester-square, London, W., in return or a Postal crder for is. ld.

ILettero to the Mitor.

BANK QUAY STATION, WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Will you kindly:publish the following letter, if your space will allow. On Monday last, seven of the men who appear to have a regular stand at the corners surrounding the above station and to the annoyance of the passers-by, were summoned by the police for ob- struction, and we wish to contradict a statement made by a certain gentleman to the effect that there were in- sufficient outside badge porters at the station and that if it were not for the men referred to 'passengers would have to wait for their luggage to be taken away. Now we wish to point out to the public that thew are six badge porters and it takes us all our time to get a living, owing to other men stopping servants who have been sent to the station by thcir master or mistress for , an outside porter,and questioning them as to their busi- ness and then informing the parties that they are outside porters. In other cases, if we happen to miss a traveller when he is leaving the platform, these men inform him that they are outside badge porters, and are engaged as such. Now this is a great injustice to us, as we have to pay for our badges. Trusting you will kindly do us this favour by publishing the above, we remain, yours sincerely,

OUTSIDE BADGE PORTERS, Bank Quay Station.

LIVE SEPULTURE : AN URGENTLY NEEDED

REFORM.

Sir.—The unreliability of the various signs of death, except"putrefaction,renders.it extremely'difficult,even for experts, to tell whether the indent is really deao. This is well illustrated by a case recently reported in the "Sheffield Daily News "where the body of a man, sup- posed to be dead, was placed in a church prior to burial, and after remaining there for several hours came to life to the consternation:of his relatives and medical adviser. Such cases are by no means rare and accentuate the dread that many people have of being buried alive. It is only a short time since a case was reported in the Press,in which a sailor's bride, living near Weymouth,fell into a trance and remained in that state for five days, causing her friendsto believe she was dead; and if pub- lie attention had not just at that period been directd to this subject of live sepulture in all probability she would have been consigned to her grave. Amongst the numer- ous historicel cases related by Dr. Franz Hirtmann in his recently published treatise is the following :—" In a small town in Prussia, an undertaker, living within the limits of the cemetery, heard during the night cries pro- ceeding from within a grave in which a person had been buried the previous day. Not daring to interfere with- out permission, he went to the police and reported the matter. When,after a great deal of delay, the required formalities were fulfilled and permission granted to open the grave, it was found that the man had been buried alive,but he was now dead. His body, which had been cold at the time of the funeral,was now warm and bleed- ing from many wounds where he had skinned his head and hands n his struggle to free himself before suffoca- tion made an end to his misery." To be forewarned is to be forearmed,and the sooner people seriously consider the danger that threatens themselves and others of being alive in a state of trance, catalepsy, or other forms of suipended animation, from whatever cause, the sooner will reforms in the examination of the supposed dead, and the granting of certificates of death, be thought necessary. It is the duty of medical practitioners to carefully examine every supposed corpse, and ascertain the fact as well as the cause of death ; and the body, should be retained in a suitable place until decomposi- tion sets in.which,as Flufeland says," is the only reliable proof tbat the vital energy has departed from an organ- ism." _ _

Women and children are particularly liable to death- counterfeits ; also the poor, who suffer from nervous ex- haustion in their endeavour to earn a living, often alas ! insufficient to keep them from such perils.—Yours faith- fully, W. R. J. 25th March, 1896.

TEE PARISH COUNCIL ELECTION.—The election of Parish Councillors was duly held on Monday from 12 o'clock noon until 8 p.m. The election was held in the Infant's School. Dignum, 66 ; Mr. E. Cross, 60; Mr. R. Garnett, 59; Mr. D. McKechnie, 59 ; Mr. A. White, 59 ; and Mr. J. Millington, 51. The/first seven were declared elected. THE PARISH MEETIISG.

Last evening week the annual parish meeting was held in the Wesleyan School. Mr. J. C. Routledge, the chair- man of the late Parish Council, occupied the chair. There were present Messrs.Parker,Millington,and Ditch- field (overseers), and a good number of parishioners. The minutes of last annual meeting having been read the Clerk submitted the accounts of receipts and expen- diture during the past half-year. On poor rate account there had been a balance at the beginning of the half- year of £92 us. 9,1. The expenditure during six months had been-2252 to union ; £136 on highway ; £25 Bs. 2d. to Rural District Council, and £2l Os. 31. for sundries. The balance now in hand on the account was £92 10s. Bd. As to special expenses' account there had been a balance at the beginning of last half year of £3B 4s. 5d.; the expenditure had been £3O, and income from rate £B4 Bs. 91, so that there was now a balance of £72 133. 2d.,which would have to Ise applied to liquidation of the cast incurred by laying la new sewer at Halton Bridge. The estimate for the forthcoming half-year was very much the same as in the past. The poor rate would be is. 2d. in the 2, and the sanitary rate 4d. in the 2. The ratable value would be as last year. Messrs. Eden and Tetlow were re-elected for the office of burleymen. Mr. White wished to know whether the Technical Schools had been a success, and Mr. Routledge replied that out of 126 candidates from all parts of England six from Penketh had passed the necessary examination in the tanning class which he thought very good.—Mr. Hobson wished to know what was to be done in regard to a mortuary for bodies found drowned or otherwise,tte the landlords in the township could refuse to take them in. The senior overseer would perhaps find one on hi. door step some morning and he would have to take charge of it until the inquest. (Langhter.)—Mr. Rout- ledge said he thought that was a question more for the Parish Council than the parish meeting. Mr. Dignum wished to know the feeling of the meet- ing as to whether they wished to extend the lighting of the parish or to discontiuue it,as the present system was voted very satisfactory.—Mr. Millington said that the lamps cost £2 18s. 6d. per lamp per year. Out the War- rington people were charging more ler lighting than other towns. He considered that the present lighting was unsatisfactory. The subject of Halton Bridge was brought forward. The fault,it was said,lay with the Warrington Highway Board. The parishioners felt very strongly in the matter and hcped something would be done.—Mr. Foster read a report of the last meeting of the Warrington District Highway Board re this question. As to the question of lighting, the Chairman asked for a vote as to whether they ought to extend the lighting or discontinue it.—Mr. Foster said the matter was one solely for the Rural District Council, and as the parish had asked for,and got the lighting,they would now have to keep and pay for it.—Mr. Parker proposed that the matter should be left over to the Parish Council,and this was carried.

THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, APRIL 4, 1896.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The Warrington team which journeyed to Brighouse on Saturday was not one which could be considered to represent the true strength of the wirepullers. There were several notable absentees, and it was hardly to be expected that the team would beat the well-known Rangers who gave such a good account of themselves when they visited Warrington earlier in the season. Nevertheless they gave a very creditable display, and at the close of the game Ow score stood :—Brighouse 1-3-0, Warrington 1-1-0. When compared with the score on the Warrington ground by the satne team, viz. :-2-1-0 to 04-0, the defeat of Saturday's does not seem of any great enormity,and considering all the circumstances it must be admitted that Warrington did well in retiring from the Rangers ground with the score as it is. The Ranger 4 had by far the best of the play, and " Demon" in his notes on the .Y,ame,says that the home team showed far more activity than their opponents,and went about their werk in a more finished manner.

It was in the forward department that the Warring_ tonia.ns were complQtely beaten,and when this happens, the play of the whole team is upset, and nay account somewhat, for the lack of finish already mentioned. This latter quality has certainly not been vary prominent in the career of the Warrington team this season, and it is to be hoped that there will te an improvement before the close. Of the individual play on Saturday, Heesem must be mentioned as one who saved his side from disaster on many occasions by his safe tackling. Bate played a brilliant game throughout, and his play was freely coal- meu.ed upon by the spectators. O'Brien, who made a successful drop at goal, had plenty to do in looking after the well-known Lewis Brooke,and this he did in a clever fashion. Of the remaining threequarters, Cross was the best. The pick of the sadly overplayed Warrington for- wards were FairhurstOerry, and Andrews.

Warrington will again journey into Yorkshire to-day (Saturday) to encounter the Leeds club. It will be remembered that Leeds got rather a fluky win when they visited Warrington on November 9th, and gained two points in the combination by scoring a solitary try to nil. Many are looking forward to Warrington being able to avenge that defeat to-div.

The team chosen to represent the " good old town " is as follows : Hee.om, full back; Broady, Barber,Cross, Lawless, three-quarters ; Bate and Higginbotham, half backs ; Nevins, .ndrews, Saunders, Swift, Hill, Fair- hurst, Berry and Deakin, forwards.

The suspension of the Wakefield Trinity ground for three weeks on account of the spectators' rouia treat- ment of the referee in the Wakefield v. HalifaX match, will prevent Warrington from meeting the Trinitarians on their own ground. This decision of the committee of the Yorkshire section of the Northern Union deprives the Wakefield club of three home matches—Batley,War- rington and Hunslet—but the club recommend that the games should be played on the Horbury ground, some three miles away. Horbury is a village club that re- cently got into slight trouble, it will be remembered, over a Northern Union player,and the locals are reported to be desirous of seeking conselation in the latter: Union's ranks. Up to the present there is no confirmation of this report, but be that as it may. the use of their ground would, of course, bring about their expulsion from the English Union.

It is highly probable that the Wakefield club will journey to Warrington. Negotiations with that object in view have been opened, and the Warrington commit- tee have offered the "gate" to the Trinitarians if they will do so. There is every prospect of their falling in with the offer,and if they do so, we shall have the unusual opportunity of seeing the Wakefield team for the second time this season on the Wilderspool enclosure. The match is fixed for next Saturday, April 11th.

Manningham were fined on Tuesday evening for being the cause of a late start when they visited Warrington. The reason for the delay was the late arrival of Brown, one of their three-quarters. But as he did good service for them, and in some measure was the cause of the team winning the match, the committee have had no qualms in paying the fine. Even their own representa- tivo on the committee voted in favour of the imposition of it.

It is satisfactory to state that the prospects of the Northern Union in the Lancashire section are particu- larly bright, as it ia a positive fact that at least five clubs in the county are in treaty with Mr. Platt. Three of the would-be entrants are now wearing the mantle of mem- bers of the Lancashire Senior Competition. Of the others one occupies a prominent position in the Second Division and the other is outside the Competitions altogether. It may be ioteresting to know that at least two of the five teams have arranged to play a match on the last Satur- day in April. There is also a lot of uneasiness in the clubs comprising the Second Division, and now they have got an example some are almost certain to follow. The Northern Union is as follows :

ASSOCIATION.

A very interesting event in local association circles was the final tie in the " Observer " cup competition on Saturday. A bright day with a stiff breeze blowing across the ground, were the conditions under which the, match was played on the cricket ground at Warrington. The St. Elphin's team and Barnton R )vers were the two competing tams and a very determined match was wit- nessed. The Rovers.won the cdp last year, and their supporters were " cock-sure " that the trophy would again go Barntonwards. Quite a large number had tra- velled from Barnton to witness the match, and they were greatly surprised at the resistance their team met with by the St. Elphin's club, which has played strongly throughout the competition. The result of an evenly contested game was a draw—two goals being scored by each, and the matck will now have to be replayed.

The holidays this year in Earlestown will follow somewhat on the same lines au previous years. The Viaduct Works and Messrs. T. and T. Vicars will close on Thursday night until Tuesday morning, the Vulcan Foundry will close at the same time,but will not re-open until Wednesday morning. In connection with the above club a shooting match for £lO a side took place on Wednesday at Haydock Park, between Mr. Rolyan and Mr. Ret- shew, members of the club. The conditions being the best out of eleven Rocks each at 24 yards rise. There was a fair attendance of spectators. Rolyan killed the first eight out of nine birds, but Retshew, only killed six out of ten, the former therefore winning easily. The annual competition for the club challenge cup, also took place, there being 11 competitor, who were handicapped according to merit. Mr. Naylor, the winner last year being placed on the scratch. The first prize was the cup and £2, second El, and third, 155., Mr. H. Hibbert and W. Thompson tied for first prize, each killing 5. On a re-shoot Hibbert won, Thompson missing his first bird. The second and third prizes were divided between J. W. Webster,T. Hamer, W. Walker, and T. Daintith. A consolation sweep was afterwards held, T. Daintith taking the first prize of 308., the second and third prizes of 20s. and lls, being divided between Messrs. Walker, Naylor, and Wakefield.

GOOD TBMPLARY.

The usual weekly session of the Hope of Earles- town Lodge of Good Template was held in the Co- opQrative Hall on Monday evening. Bro. J.Hatton, P.C.T. presided over a good attendance of members, and a number of visitors, the latter including Bro. C. H. Griffiths, District Lodge Organist, of Liver- pool. Arrangements were made for members to attend the Good Templars' demonstrations at Southport on Saturday next in connection with the juvenile section of the order, and on Easter Monday under the auspices of the Grand Lodge of England, which holds its annual session during Easter week. One candidate was admitted by card. The pro- gramme of the evening was in the hands of Bro. G. E. Warburton, chap., who gave a very interesting address on the good templet's obligations, &c.,:fol- lowered by duets, songs, and recitations, in which Sisters Mrs. Cutler, Mrs. Willis, M. Jackson, A. Jackson, A. Newman, and Bros. C. Drew and John Warburton took part, the latter being especially effective, receiving a well-merited encore. Bro. Griffiths, D.L.O. gave a short address expressing the pleasure it gave him on his first visit to the lodge, and intimated his intention of visiting them again a fortnight hence. Thanks were tendered to Bro. Griffiths, who was asked to convey greetings to the "Progressive Mission" Lodge,Liverpool,with which he was connected. A very encouraging ses- sion was then closed. SAD DROWNING CAS AT EARLBSTOWN.

On Sunday afternoon a boy, nine years of age. named John Sturgess, son of Mr. George Sturgess of 19, Regent-st., lost his life in the Sankey Brook through endeavouring to save his youngsr brother who had fallen into the water. It appears the deceased lad John, with his brother Alfred aged seven, and two other boys were playing near the Sankey Brook, on the opposite side of the canal from Vitriol Square. The brook, owing no doubt to the heavy rains, was much swollen and was run- ning very swiftly. Alfred in trying to reach a stick from the water fell into the brook, and the deceased in trying to save him fell in also. The unfortunate boys were quickly carried along, but a young man named George Glynn, jumped into the dirty water, and at great risk succeeded in rescuing the younger lad who had first fallen in. He was unable to res- cue the other lad owing to the strong current, and he was carried a distance of 300 yards down the stream until checked by some wire. Fifteen minutes had elapsed before the poor lad was taken from the water by James Powell but he was then quite dead, and the body was removed home. Had it not been for the prompt and courageous action of Glynn, it is certain both lads would have been drowned. The boy Alfred suffered much from the immersion, but has now recovered. An inquest was held at the Griffin Hotel, Earlestown, on Tuesday afternoon by Mr. Brighouse, coroner. The facts, as stated above, were deposed to,and the jury returned a verdict of" accidentally drowned." The Coroner took occasion to compliment the man Glynn, upon his bravery in rescuing at the peril of his own life, the young lad Sturgess.

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTIONS.

The polling for the 'election of Councillors in the con- tested wards of Crow-lane, Viaduct and Wargrave, took place on Monday. The poll opened at twelve o'clock, but throughout the afternoon matters were very tame, and beyond the fact that there was much vehicular traffic converging on the three polling stations, there was nothing to denote that an interesting struggle was in progress. The polling stations were Crow-lane Ward, (Wesleyan School) ; Viaduct Ward, (District school) ; Wargrave Ward, (Wargrave school) ; and the candidates were: Crow-lane, Mr. J. Smith,and Mr. M. McDermott. Viaduct, Mr. P. MacMahon, Mr. J. Clarke, and Mr. J. Carlin. Wargrave, Mr. W. Thompson and Mr. W. Crawford. Every effort was made to poll every available voter and conveyances were freely used, the result be- ing that in every case, there was a heavy poll,, the heavi- est voting taking place between 5.30 and eight o'clock, at which time the poll closed. All the ballot boxes were conveyed to the Town Hall, where under the presidency of Mr. R. Brierley as returning officer, the votes were counted. During the period of waiting for the result, a large crowd gathered in front of the Town Hall, who in- dulged in a little partisan cheering and groaning. Shortly after nine o'clock, the counting was completed, and after announcing the result in the room,Mr. Briorley announced them from the balcony, to the crowd outside, as follows.

CROW-LANE WARD.

Smith McDermott

Majority for Smith 45 WARGRAVE WARD.

Thompson Crawford! 134

The crowd cheered loudly as each result was made known, and at the close Mr. Carlin stepped on to the balcony with the intention of addressing the people,but they refused to give him a beating. Messrs. Thompson and Smith proceeded to the Conservative Club, where there was a large gathering, and a meeting for the pur- pose of expressing thanks was held. MEETING AT THE CONSERVATIVE CLUB.

Mr. J. Catterall (chairman of the Club Committee), presided, and besides a large number of members,there were present Messrs. C. B. F. Borron, James Smith, W. Collingwood, W. Thompson, R. Barton, J. Simpsoa, W. Mills, Dr. Pattison and others.

The Chairman said they had met cn a most important occasion that evening. There had been a hard contest fought in throe wards, upon grounds, not political, but upon what they considered just and right. (Applause.) He was glad to say that he had no knowledge of Mr. Thompson's political opinions, as he never knew him to give a pol.tical vote. As chairman of the Committee, he had worked zealously for Mr. Thompson, and he was proud of the glorious result.He (the speaker) had been a resident of Wargrave Ward for a great many years, but he was happy to say,that up to the present the ward had done its duty manfully and well. Be had always found Mr. Thompson a gentleman in the proper sense of the word, liberal and business like. That was more than he could say for some of the members of the Council.(Hear, hear.) _ _ _

Mr. James Smith (Crow-lane Ward) said his duty was to thank the electors of Crow-lane Ward, for the magni- ficent victory they had achieved that day. He felt now, that his con-duct in the Council was appreciated, at all events by the Crow-lane electors. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Thompson, thanked the electors of the Wargrave Ward, for the great honour they had done him by re- turning him by such a substantial and decisive majority He had to thank the electors for this expression of con- fidence which could not be interpreted,on that particular occasion, other than as an appreciation of his conduct during the past 15 months. Mr. Mills as chairman of the 'Viaduct Ward Commit- tee.was pleased at the result which had been obtained in the three elections,but he was greatly disappointed in the ward which he had been chairman of for the past 15 months.

Dr. Patterson also spoke as chairman of the Orow-lane Ward Committee.

Mr. C. B.F. Borron welcomed the return of Mr. Smith and Mr. Thompson to the Board, because during the past year,as public men, they had exhibited themselves as straightforward men, doing the business of the Coun- cil with courtesy. No business could be done unless confidence was placed, and courtesy and good feeling was exhibited on the Board.

Mr. R. Barton proposed a vote of thanks to the Chair- man which Mr. Smith seconded, and the vote was heartily accorded.

HEALTH OF RITICT-CORN DTSTRTCT.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S ANNUAL REPORT.

Dr. J. Adams, medical officer to the Runcorn District Council has published an exhaustive report on the sani- tary condition of the Rural Sanitary District for the year 1895 :—ln the old district the area is set down at 45,941 acres and in the enlarged district 49,309 acres. The population in the two districts taken by the 1891 census was 22,461, and 24,665 and the estimated popula-' tion taken in the middle of 1895 is put down as 23,594 In the old district and 26,052 in the enlarged district. During the year ending December 31st, 1895, 651 births (old district 629) and 401 deaths (old district 390) were registered as having occurred within the rural district. The figures compared with last year and referring only to the old district show a decrease of 10 births and an in- crease of 31 deaths. These figures, however, include 22 deaths at the Dutton Workhouse, twelve of which are described as belonving to Runcorn Urban District and if these are deducted from the 390 deaths in the old die- d ict they will make a total number of 388 for the entire district. Of the 388 deaths no less than 34 formed the subject of coroners' enquiries and an examination of the inquests showed the causes of death to be :—Drowning, 9 cases; burns,&c.,4 cases ; suffocation,3 cases; accidents, 7 cases, and natural causes 11. Of the births 337 were males, and 314 females,and the deaths were made up by 207 males and 194 females. The birth rata was 24-9 per 1,000 per annum for the old district and it may be in- teresting to note that this is by far the lowest birth rate recorded during a period of ten years the previous lowest one being 26.3 in 1887. The death rate was 14.9 per 1,000 per annum against an average for the last ten years of 17.4. In the list showing deaths from zymotic diseases there is set down to scarlet fever five, diphtheria two, measles 16, whooping cough:three, diarrhcea 20, rheumatic fever, four. With reference to the provision of an Isolation Hospital Dr. Adams writes" This question of isolation of infectious diseases within the area of this District Council should soon assume a definite shape. The figures of my statis- tical tables point to the need for some accommodation being provided. The late Sanitary Authority, by resolu- tion, passed and recorded on its minute Book, a decision to erect an Infectious Diseases Hospital. The matter should be taken up again, in spite of the refusal of the Local Government Board to sanction the sites formerly proposed. I would suggest that a strong committee be appointed, or that the existing Committee on this and cognate matters be instructed by the District Council to consider the relative advis- ability of one single hospital being provided, or of a aerie!' of smaller hospitals being erected, say, one in each registration sub-district, or of single cottages or houses already in existence being purchased or rented for this purpose, or of making arrangements with the Chester' Warrington and Northwich Authorities respec- tively, for the admission to their infectious diseases hospitals of those cases which may occur nearest to th ir borders. I shall be only too glad to meet a committee appointed for this purpose, and to obtain for them all the information I can command." Dr. Adams in his account of enquiries made into sanitary matters con- cludes by saying "Nothing special calls for note; no large extensions have been undertaken. By the proposed closing of the tidal openings on the Manchester Ship Canal there is a risk of serious interference with your sewer outfalle along the line of the Ship Canal ; this matter should receive your grave attention."

WESTON.

PUBLIC-11011SE Trreirr.—Before Messrs. Norman and Imison, on Wednesday morning, at the Runcorn Police Station. Mary Whitehead, Heath-r3ad. Weston, was charged with stealing a bottle of beer valued at 3d. from the Quarry Hotel, Weston, on Tuesday.—Oliver Dun- bavin's evidence tended to show that prisoner went to his house on Tuesday evening and asked for half a pint of beer. While he (the landlord) went into the cellar to draw it he saw her putting something under her apron. He asked what she had got, and prisoner in reply said "Nothing." On insisting he found she had a bottle of beer, and he therefore sent for the police.—P.C. Morris said he was called to the hotel and charged the prisoner with stealing a bottle of beer. Prisoner in answer to the charge said she was tipsy and did not know what she was doing.—Mr. Dunbavin said that prisoner had been bringing empty beer bottles back regularly, and this had aroused his suspicions.—A fine of 53. including costs or seven days was inflicted.

NOTES AND NEWS.

Mr. Joel Chandler Harris, the well-known American writer, the author of "Uncle Remus," is one of the most modern of men. He shuns the interviewer,and any sort of lionising fills him with trepidation. Some time ago the author's club in New York gave him a reception. It was larger than he anticipated, and in the midst of it all he disappeared. His greatest passion is roses, and in the early morning,when the dew is on the grass,one can hear the snipping of the pruning-shears as Mr. Harris works in his garden—the finest rose-garden in Atlanta.

General Weyler, the so•called Spanish " butcher," is described as being " only of medium height, broad- shouldered, stockily built and muscular. His face is a remarkable study in itself. The head is large, and all its features prominent. Sparce, once dark hair, now turning to an iron grey, meets a broad, receding fore- head. There are tense and sharp lines and furrows just over the bridge of the nose, the latter the most pro- minent feature of the face. Shrewd, cold, glinting eyes are set far back in the sockets, under bushy eyebrows. The nose is strong and masterful. A broad thin-lipped mouth is not at all hidden by the thin moustache that is supplemented by luxurious, well-cropped side- whiskers,also tinged with grey and framing in the hard stern chin."

The Transatlantic cyclist is pushing ahead. The other day proposals were noted for special cyclist tracks along all new roads in some United States and Canad- ian cities, and now we see that a Bill is befor. the New York Legislature which makes it a penal offence to drop or place, or cause to be placed upon any cycle path, street, sidewalk, road, &c., any substance which might injure or puncture any tyre used on a bicycle. Does the inclusion of sidewalks in the Bill foreshadow the time when the footpath will be no longer free from the wheelmen

The company for selling liquor on the Gothenburg system at Bergen had a narrow escape last week. This town.with its 60,000 inhabitants, is the second town of Norway. 14,172 voted against the company, 14,590 voted for it or did not vote. Non-voters are reckoned as though they vote yes. The agitation was very ani- mated. In Christiania, where there is no contest, the company recently distributed £23,000 as its net receipt for the year 1895. The town got £6,000, three parks £5OO, a fountain fund £5OO (now in all £1,800), a fund for park music £3OO, Children's Asylums £1,500, the Museum for Artistic Industry £9oo,the National Theatre for its new building £2,700 (siKtli donation), the National Theatre for other purposes £6OO, the Work- men's Academy £5OO, the Salvation Army £3OO, and several hospitals £2,000, Sze.

Scotland does not possess an uncontested pre-eminence in the gentle art of heckling Parliamentary candidates. Mr. John M‘Gillicuddy, the Tory candidate for East Kerry,at a meeting held in the village of Fierin the other day, experienced a somewhat unpleasant discomfiture when attempting to raise a laugh against a heckler. " Will you abolish the House of Lords ?" was the query addressed to the candidate by a respectable local farmer. " I will," immediately answered Mr. M'Gillicuddy, "of course, abolish the House of Lords when you get into it." Instantly (says a" Daily News "report) caine the rejoinder.addressed to the crowd, " Boys, have you heard what he has said ? Be tells you he will support the House of Lords so long as it is a house of landlords, but the moment the people get into it he will at once abolish it.

Much has been printed about Mr. Chamberlain and his orchids. One fact mentioned in the April " Cassell's Magazine" may, however, not be generally known, and that is that about twenty gardeners are usually employed at Highbury, the care of the orchids making the demand of the whole of the time of three of the number. For the welfare of these plants an experienced orchidist is held responsible; but Mr. Chamberlain himself still contrives tc take a keen personal interest in their cultivation. On his Saturday-to-Monday visits, and when residing at Highbury during the Parliamentary recess, the best part of his little leisure is spent in the plant-houses. Every I,plant is numbered according to a private catalogue the 'right hon. gentleman himself keeps, and the gardeners declare that every one of the five or six thousand spai- mens at flighbury is individually known to him.

An interesting Australian lady now visiting London is Mrs.Rowan,a sister of Lady Charles Kerr. She has been a great traveller, and has visited parts of Australia and New Zealand very little known even to the most intrepid pioneers, and she has also crossed the Himalayas. Her object in travel has been to reproduce in water-colours the flora of the regions she has visited,and an exhibition of her pictures will take place early in April, at Dowdes- well's Galleries, in London. In person Mrs. Rowan is a very slight woman, whose fragile physique seems al- most incompatible with the hardships she has had to undergo in following her pursuit of rare flowers. Amongst the most beautiful of her pictures is one of the dreaded nettle plant of Tasmania—a skrub which is said to pro- duce instantaneous death to the man or beast who has the misfortune to be scratched, however slightly, by its almost invisible thorns. The plant has a soft velvety leaf and a lovely white flower.

Whilst we are taking our census in England. precisely the same periodical operation is going on in France, and by a curious chance amongst the population of the Re public will be reckoned on this occasion the Queen of England, the King of the Belgians, the Dowager Empress of Russia, the heirs apparent or prospective to the throne of England, Russia, and Austria, the Grand Duke and Grand Duchess of Mecklenburg-Schweritz, the Dike of OldPnburg, and the Duke of Leuchtenberg. Four princesses of our royal house—namely, Princess Chris- tian, Princess Louise' Princess Beatrice, and the Duchess of Albany fall under the same category, as do also the Grand Duke Michael and the Grand Duchess Olga of Russia, the Archduchess Elizabeth of Austria, and Prin cess Clementine of Belgium. Among personages only a shade less illustrious, enjoying at this moment French hospitality, are the Grand Dukes Alexis and Peter, the Archdukes Rainer, Otho, and Charles Ferdinand, and Prince and Princess Philip of Saxe-Coburg. The Em- press of Austria and Mr. Gladstone have just escaped being added to the list, but the Empress EugiSnie, Lord Salisbury. and Nubar Pasha will figure in the roll-call of the great democracy.

Whilst the battle-cry of" America for the American" Is rousing mighty echoes in the world of politics, a new doctrine has been formulated in the words "the American woman for the American man." It advocates declare that it is an injury to the material interests of the natien,and an insult to the rising manhood of vigor- ous and cultured people, that every girl with a few millions of dollars for her dowry should be snapped up by some impoverished representative of an effete Euro- pean aristocracy. The system amounts to absenteeism in its worst shape, for not only does it sink the capital drawn from the native soil, or filched from native labour, in the maintenance of a class detested and abol- ished by Republicans, but it carries off across the ocean the sower and prime of American womanhood, while nothing is given in return, except a few titles to dress out newspaper paragraphs. It is hardly a matter for, wonder, then, that a proposition should be seriously mooted in the United States for imposing a fifty percent.' tax on the dowers of brides who bestow themselves on alien husbands.

Mr. Hogan has addressed several questions to Mr. Chamberlain lately on the subject of the future govern- ment of Norfolk Island, which is peopled mainly by the de,cendants of the mutineers of the Bounty. For the past 40 years (says the "Times") the Norfolk Islanders have been, to all intents and purpeses, a little republic in the Pacific, but thoroughly loyal subjects of Her Majesty. Every Boxing-day they meet and choose their chief magistrate for the ensuing year. Mr. Chamberlain now proposes to place them under the direct authority of the Government and Parliament of New South Wales. Judging from the Australian papers just received, the proposed change does not meet with general approval. Mr. Stephen Adams, the chief magistrate of Norfolk Island for the current year, was in Sydney when the mail left, discussing the situation with Lord Hampden, the Governor of New South Wales. He is reported to have soid :—" We want to continue as we are. We res- pect New South Wales, but we prefer the rule of the Imperial Government, which has been just because it has left us alone." This is corroborated by the Norfolk Island correspondent of the "Sydney Morning Herald," who writes :—" Generally speaking, the change, if decided upon, will be distasteful to the inhabitants."

The railway companies continue to give the farmers less reason to grumble. At a meeting at Charing Cross Hotel, Sir G. Russell, chairman of the South Eastern Railway Company, announced a reduction of 15 per cent on the present rates of fruits and vegetables from all stations to London, various reductions on most classes Of manures. and 15 per cent on cattle feeding stuffs ; while Mr. Willis, manager of the Company's passenger department,stated that they were negotiating with °the companies for reduced fruit rates to the N!,rth of Eng- land, and had agreed with the Chatham and Dow/. and Brighton companies to reduce the rates on Milk, and to bring within the operation of the rates for small parcels carried at owner's risk fresh butter. cream, fresh meat, dead poultry and rabbits, and other articles. Hearty thanks were tenriered the Company for the concessions made.

WESTON. Considerable interest was manifested in the election of members for the Parish Council of Weston ward. The election took place on Saturday, the polling booth being located in the Girl's School, under the presiding officer- ship of Mr. F. Johnson, with Mr. J. Collier as poll clerk. The result of the count was declared as follows: The Rev. Canon A. Maitland Wood, 89 : Messrs. Joseph Tudor, 85 ; James Ford, 85 ; John Ball, 78; Thomas T. Sill, 72 ; David Somerville, 70 ' • Arthur G. Guest, 65,and Daniel Basnett, 61 ; (elected). Messrs. W. Dunbavin,6l ; Thomas Griffiths, 52. and J. Ellis Sweetman, 31. It is worthy of note that Messrs. Basnett and Dunbavin each received 61 votes. To meet such a result the presiding officer was empowered to decide by casting lots,and this being done the election was decided in favour of Mr. IBasnett. HELSBY. C. Taylor, 80 ; A. J. Briant, 76 ; J. T. Collier, 70; W. Barlow. 68; J. Potts, 66; G. White, 66 • and J. Brandreth, 58; (all elected) Messrs. W. Guest, 51; T. Noden, 50; and S. Moss, 45. Mr. G. F. Ashton was the presiding officer. ALVANLEY. The result of the:Parish Council electic n was as follows —Messrs. A. Greenway, 38 ; Thcmas Wright, 35; W. H. Lowe. 33 ; S. H. Noden, 32 ' • T.
